11 Edge Intelligence

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Fleet stack focus for this chapter.

When does learning need to move from the data center to the devices it serves?

A model trained in a data center encounters new conditions when deployed to a user’s device. Vocabulary, lighting, acoustics, and usage patterns may differ from the distributions that shaped its parameters, and the gap can widen as behavior evolves. Centralized training cannot anticipate every context, while privacy constraints or limited connectivity may prevent local observations from reaching it. Learning where the data is produced can improve local behavior, but running predictions offline does not by itself justify training there. Adaptation must provide enough benefit to justify its memory, battery, and thermal costs without disrupting the device’s primary work. A training job that fits on an idle phone may become unacceptable when the user starts another application. Even successful local learning leaves a fleet problem: improvements must be evaluated across devices whose capabilities, data, and availability differ. A policy that accepts updates only from well-connected devices may improve measured results while neglecting users whose devices rarely qualify. Making one device learn efficiently therefore does not establish that a population can learn reliably or that exchanging model updates protects private information. The engineering decision joins the value of local adaptation with the cost of participation and the evidence needed to trust shared improvements. In C³ terms, compute must fit each device, communication must carry useful updates within limited connectivity windows, and coordination must preserve a representative learning population despite dropout and unequal resources.

Learning Objectives
  • Contrast centralized cloud training, on-device learning, and federated learning by analyzing their data flow, privacy properties, and operational trade-offs
  • Quantify edge-device training overhead relative to inference-only deployment across memory, compute, and energy
  • Derive an admission condition for background adaptation from energy, thermal, and foreground-latency slack
  • Select model-adaptation and data-efficiency methods by the memory term each shrinks, its forgetting risk, and its storage cost
  • Analyze how client sampling, dropout, and non-IID data determine which population a federated model learns from and its time to target
  • Design federated learning systems that aggregate privacy-preserving updates within uplink, secure-aggregation, and version-compatibility limits
  • Explain the operational controls needed for on-device learning: device-aware deployment, distributed validation, privacy-preserving monitoring, and rollback across heterogeneous populations
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

11.1 The Edge Learning Paradigm

Imagine a voice assistant trained on millions of generic audio samples in a cloud data center. When deployed, it may struggle with a regional accent or household vocabulary. Without local adaptation, its performance can decline as usage diverges from the training distribution. Edge intelligence places inference, adaptation, and coordination on or near the devices that observe the data, allowing models to respond to local context under power, memory, privacy, and connectivity constraints.

The serving fleet already has to meet latency targets despite failures and uneven demand. At the edge, the operator also loses control over when a device has power, connectivity, or spare memory. The unit of design remains the fleet, but its participants can disappear between selection and completion. Local adaptation matters here because its cost determines which users can contribute to population learning.

↩︎ Serving fleets that hold latency service-level objectives (SLOs) through replica failures and uneven demand are established in Serving at Scale.

Definition 11.1: On-device learning

On-device learning is the local training or adaptation of machine learning models directly on deployed hardware, using data that remains on the device; a local update needs no server connection, although sharing its result does.

  1. Significance: It enables personalization and continued adaptation where connectivity is intermittent or raw data may not leave the device. In single-machine iron-law terms, each update adds backward and optimizer work to the compute term \(\Operations/(\PeakRate \cdot \EfficiencyHw)\) that foreground inference also needs, and it draws on a separate battery and thermal budget that equation 2 makes explicit.
  2. Distinction: Unlike edge inference, which only executes a fixed model, on-device learning runs a local optimization loop (forward and backward passes plus parameter updates) that requires more memory and compute than the forward pass alone.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that on-device learning requires training from scratch. In reality, it is almost always on-device adaptation, which fine-tunes a small subset of a pretrained base model’s parameters to fit the data distribution of a local environment.

Consider one federated round for the voice assistant. A coordinator selects eligible devices and distributes a shared model version and a bounded training policy. Each selected device trains on local examples when its policy admits background work, then returns a model update tagged with the starting version. Some devices lose connectivity or exhaust their resource allowance. The coordinator closes the round according to its deadline and participation requirements, combines compatible updates, evaluates the candidate shared model, and publishes an accepted version for later download. Local personalization can remain on the device instead of entering that aggregate.

This round has the shape of Archetype C (Federated MobileNet), with phone-class clients, private local data, and intermittent availability, and it exposes the C³ trade-off before any adaptation method is chosen. The Fleet Law (equation 1.3) gives it a time budget, with one change of reading. In a data center the compute term divides one job’s work across \(\DeviceCount\) devices. Here every selected device trains its own copy on its own data, so the term is set by local training on the slowest device the round still waits for rather than divided by \(\DeviceCount\). The communication term is the update transfer over a wide-area uplink. The synchronization term is the wait for enough participants to report. The overlap term credits whatever downloading and training proceed concurrently. More local compute can reduce the frequency of communication, but devices then need longer uninterrupted training windows and their models can drift toward different local distributions. Short deadlines improve coordination speed while excluding slow participants. The fleet must therefore account for whose updates complete, rather than merely how many devices were selected. Device budgets, adaptation methods, and data reuse determine the work inside the round; aggregation and scheduling determine whether that work improves the shared model. A round of this kind, repeated across a population, is federated learning.

↩︎ Archetype C (Federated MobileNet), the phone-class federated workload with private local data and intermittent availability, is established in Archetype C (Federated MobileNet).

Definition 11.2: Federated learning

Federated learning is a decentralized training paradigm where distributed devices collaboratively train a shared model using local data while exchanging only model updates (gradients or weights).

  1. Significance: It transforms the constraint of data locality into a privacy feature. Within the Fleet Law (equation 1.3), federated learning is constrained by wide-area bandwidth (\(\Bandwidth\)) in its communication term and by the extreme heterogeneity of the fleet, where device-specific efficiency \((\EfficiencyHw)\) and availability can vary by orders of magnitude.
  2. Distinction: Unlike centralized training, where data is moved to the compute, federated learning moves compute to the data. Raw training examples can remain local, but model updates, participation metadata, and protocol messages still cross the device boundary and may carry personal information.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that federated learning is “inherently private.” In reality, model updates themselves can leak information through gradient inversion attacks, which search for inputs whose gradient matches a submitted update and so reconstruct training examples, requiring additional protections like differential privacy or secure aggregation.

Keeping raw examples local reduces one source of exposure, but an update can still reveal information about the examples that produced it. Secure aggregation lets the coordinator obtain a combined update without inspecting each client’s contribution under the protocol’s trust assumptions. Differential privacy1 bounds how much a specified user’s or record’s participation can change the released result, usually by limiting contributions and adding calibrated noise. Neither protection follows automatically from federation. Their costs and assumptions belong in the round design alongside dropout handling and communication.

1 Differential privacy: A mathematical framework that bounds information leakage by adding calibrated noise to computations. In edge learning, that noise becomes a systems cost because noisier updates usually require more rounds or more data to reach the same utility.

↪ Differential privacy definitions, noise mechanisms, and privacy accounting are developed in Differential Privacy.

A smartphone predicting user text input, a smart home device responding to household routines, and an autonomous vehicle interpreting local driving conditions can all encounter variation that centralized training data underrepresents. The smartphone meets user-specific phrasing. The home device observes seasonal shifts in household routines. The vehicle faces local road conditions, weather patterns, and traffic habits that differ from its training environment. Such gaps make a case for on-device learning (definition 11.1), moving machine learning from centralized training to distributed learning across heterogeneous devices that operate under tight power, memory, and thermal limits. Because local training spends those scarce device budgets, a distribution gap is a case for adaptation rather than a verdict; an engineering analysis must first establish when local learning is necessary and whether a given update measurably improves behavior.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A mobile robotics vision system must process each frame within \(33\text{ ms}\), while cloud network round-trip time is \(80\text{–}150\text{ ms}\). Why must the foreground perception path execute locally even if model training remains in the cloud?

    1. Network round-trip latency alone exceeds the foreground deadline, so a frame cannot wait for a cloud response.
    2. Cloud data centers are physically incapable of computing backpropagation gradients on batch sizes of one within milliseconds.
    3. The on-device camera sensor cannot serialize video frames into digital packets without first running an on-device forward-backward training pass.
    4. On-device adaptation reduces model parameter count to zero before sending updates across the cellular uplink.

  2. Before investing in an on-device gradient-based learning pipeline for a mobile news app, an ML systems architect considers feature-based personalization (local feature caching with a frozen global model) versus on-device gradient fine-tuning. Under what systems criteria is feature-based personalization preferred, and what eliminates the need for local backpropagation?

  3. In comparing learning paradigms across edge fleets, what fundamental systems distinction separates isolated on-device learning from federated learning?

    1. Isolated on-device learning transmits raw private data to centralized servers, whereas federated learning processes data locally without communication.
    2. Isolated on-device learning restricts parameter updates entirely to a single node, preventing collective fleet knowledge sharing, whereas federated learning coordinates model updates across devices while preserving data locality.
    3. Federated learning trains models from scratch on client devices without a pretrained base model, whereas isolated on-device learning requires continuous cloud synchronization.
    4. Isolated on-device learning uses only INT4 integer arithmetic, whereas federated learning requires double-precision FP64 hardware accelerators on all mobile clients.

  4. True or False: Because on-device learning keeps raw user observations (such as typed keystrokes or biometric sensor traces) on the client device, it inherently guarantees full compliance with privacy regulations like GDPR and HIPAA without requiring any additional cryptographic or governance safeguards.

  5. Compare the coordination responsibilities of isolated on-device learning, centralized cloud training, and cross-device federated learning. Why does the paradigm alone not establish a universal ranking of implementation complexity?

See Answers →

11.2 Why Learn on the Device

A distribution gap justifies spending device resources only when centralized retraining cannot close it in time or within policy. A cloud-trained model that relies on a server round trip or a raw-data upload for every adjustment fails whenever a user has an unfamiliar accent, a remote sensor loses connectivity, a regulation forbids exporting personal traces, or millions of devices would overwhelm backend ingestion. Those four pressures (personalization, latency and availability, privacy, and infrastructure efficiency) motivate shifting learning to the edge (Li et al. 2020). Personalization matters when deployed models encounter usage patterns and data distributions that their training environments represent poorly. Local adaptation can refine behavior using user-specific data, including linguistic preferences, physiological baselines, sensor characteristics, or environmental conditions. High inter-user variability motivates testing this approach against a fixed model with local context; the measured quality difference determines whether changing weights is worthwhile.

Latency and availability constraints determine where an update can run and how soon it can become useful. In edge computing scenarios, connectivity to centralized infrastructure may be unreliable, delayed, or intentionally limited to preserve bandwidth or reduce energy consumption. When adaptation is justified, local learning permits updates during disconnected periods. The update must still fit the device’s resource budget, and its completion deadline is separate from the response deadline for foreground inference.

Privacy is the third driver. Many applications involve sensitive or regulated data including biometric measurements, typed input, location traces, or health information. Local learning can reduce privacy exposure by keeping raw data on the device and limiting transmission to centralized systems. This can simplify compliance engineering, but it does not by itself establish adherence to regulations such as the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR), the Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act (HIPAA) (U.S. Department of Health and Human Services 2026), or region-specific data sovereignty laws.

U.S. Department of Health and Human Services. 2026. The HIPAA Security Rule. HHS.gov guidance.

Infrastructure efficiency is the economic driver. Centralized training pipelines require backend infrastructure to collect, store, and process user data from potentially millions of devices. Shifting learning to the edge distributes computation and can reduce raw-data ingestion. The resulting savings must be compared with device energy, repeated model transfers, and coordination overhead; whether moving work off the server lowers end-to-end cost depends on that whole sum, not on the server bill alone.

11.2.1 Simpler alternatives first

Before paying that device and coordination cost, an engineering team should test whether a static model paired with local state already meets the requirement. Shipping an on-device gradient-training loop when a local feature store or lookup table suffices spends memory, battery, and rollback margin for no measurable gain, whereas simpler alternatives often achieve comparable quality with far lower operational overhead. Three such alternatives often satisfy personalization and adaptation requirements without the complexity of local training (table 1).

The decision to implement on-device learning should follow evidence that simpler alternatives cannot meet the deployment requirements, such as restrictions on transferring training data or unreliable connectivity when adaptation is needed. Latency is the requirement most often mistaken for such evidence. For applications whose foreground deadlines are shorter than the network round-trip time, cloud inference is architecturally infeasible. Camera processing under 33 ms, voice response under 500 ms, AR/VR motion-to-photon latency under 20 ms, or safety-critical control under 10 ms all face network round-trip times typically ranging from 50 to 200 ms. When the network cannot meet the foreground deadline, inference must remain local. A fixed model also executes offline, however, so the deadline settles where the model runs and leaves open where it learns. Local training earns its engineering investment only when a measured adaptation gain justifies its added resource and operational costs.

11.2.2 Where adaptation pays

Every application domain must justify adaptation against its simpler alternatives, and the domains differ in which resource binds first. A smartphone keyboard encounters personal vocabulary and changing typing patterns that may be poorly represented in its released model. Local training can use those observations for personalization or contribute updates to a shared model; these are separate objectives. Google’s Gboard,2 for instance, employs federated learning to improve shared models across a large population of users while keeping raw data local to each device (Hard et al. 2018).

2 Gboard (2017): One of the first widely reported commercial federated learning deployments at mobile scale. Published reports showed that keyboard models could improve from aggregated on-device updates while raw typed text remained local. The systems consequence is durable. Compressed updates and privacy mechanisms make cross-fleet learning possible over constrained mobile networks without centralizing raw keystrokes.

Hard, Andrew, Kanishka Rao, Rajiv Mathews, Swaroop Ramaswamy, Françoise Beaufays, Sean Augenstein, Hubert Eichner, Chloé Kiddon, and Daniel Ramage. 2018. “Federated Learning for Mobile Keyboard Prediction.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1811.03604.

The same keyboard also runs two interactive prediction strategies at inference time (figure 1). Next-word prediction ranks discrete candidate tokens (“favorite,” “address,” “schedule”) in the suggestion bar, whereas Smart Compose generates multi-word inline ghost text (“ail address”) through on-the-fly rescoring directly inside the input buffer. Both mechanisms personalize the immediate interaction through local context; longer-lived model improvement occurs separately when federated training aggregates eligible on-device updates.

Wearable health monitors, which track activity and physiology from accelerometers, heart rate sensors, and electrodermal activity monitors, face a different personalization problem. Individual physiological baselines and sensor placement can affect predictions, motivating calibration or local adaptation. Evaluation must distinguish useful personalization from learning away a change the application is meant to detect. A monitor that adapts to a user’s steadily rising resting heart rate has normalized the very signal it exists to flag. Keeping observations local reduces raw-data transfer; whether the adapted model is more accurate is a separate measurement against held-out outcomes.

Voice interfaces in smart speakers and earbuds must recognize commands quickly and accurately in noisy, changing acoustic environments, starting with wake-word detection.3 These systems face foreground latency requirements, such as an end-to-end response target under 500 ms and a wake-word detection target below 100 ms. Those targets constrain inference and the interference permitted from background learning. Local adaptation addresses a separate quality objective: reducing false activations and missed detections for a user’s voice and acoustic environment. Far-field microphone configurations and room acoustics motivate that adaptation, but its value must be measured while preserving foreground response times.

3 Wake-word detection: An always-on detector incurs inference cost throughout the listening period, even when no keyword occurs. Published keyword-spotting results on dedicated neural processors, plotted in figure 6, spend on the order of 30 to 160 \(\Glyph{\mu}\)J per evaluation, so the energy budget is set by how often the detector evaluates over the listening period as much as by the cost of any one evaluation. Local personalization adds a separate update cost and earns its place only if evaluation shows an acceptable trade-off between missed keywords, false activations, and energy use.

Industrial IoT and remote monitoring systems expose the availability side of the decision. In agricultural sensing, pipeline monitoring, or environmental surveillance, connectivity to centralized infrastructure may be limited, expensive, or unavailable. Local inference permits continued detection while disconnected. Local learning adds the ability to adjust thresholds or track seasonal trends, provided the update preserves the distinction between expected variation and anomalies. The consequences of missed detections make validation and a recoverable baseline part of the adaptation design.

Embedded computer vision systems, including robotics, AR/VR, and smart cameras, combine substantial visual processing with the tight foreground deadlines of section 11.2.1, from a 33 ms frame interval down to a sub-10 ms control loop. On-device adaptation can recalibrate a model to new lighting conditions, object appearances, or motion patterns, but it spends resources needed by that foreground pipeline. A useful update must improve validated behavior without exhausting the latency margin that made local inference viable.

Each domain turns a possible adaptation benefit into a resource decision. Mobile keyboards must fit update state alongside typing, wearables must budget energy across repeated updates, voice and vision systems must preserve foreground deadlines, and disconnected sensors must decide whether a fixed model remains adequate.

11.2.3 What crosses the device boundary

Once a domain justifies local learning, the next question is whether each device learns in isolation or shares what it discovers with the fleet. Keeping all learning strictly inside each device prevents a fleet from sharing new words, road hazards, or acoustic patterns discovered across millions of users, yet uploading raw observations to a central cluster exposes private data and consumes wide-area bandwidth. Centralized training collects data on managed infrastructure and releases validated model versions to devices; local adaptation instead changes a device’s model using observations that may be unavailable centrally. Hardware, software, power, and connectivity then vary across the learning population, so an operator must coordinate model versions and evaluate updates from intermittently participating clients (Bonawitz et al. 2019).

A returning device may hold an old model or fail to apply a release. Signed updates, functional checks, deployment telemetry, and rollback therefore form part of the learning protocol (Bonawitz et al. 2019). The central decision is how much state to share across devices. Centralized training, local-only adaptation, and federated coordination are architectural choices that can coexist in one deployment.

Local-only adaptation specializes a model to its user’s observations, which are often non-IID4 and noisy, so sharing that specialization requires an explicit boundary between personal state and the parameters intended to improve the population model. Federated coordination adds population learning by periodically aggregating updates to shared parameters. A deployment must specify which parameters enter the aggregate and which remain personal; averaging every local adaptation would erase that distinction. Data locality limits raw-data transfer, while separate privacy controls govern the updates that leave each device. The three architectures therefore differ in what crosses the device boundary, not in how advanced they are (figure 2): local-only learning sends nothing, centralized training sends raw examples, and federated learning sends only model updates \(\FiniteChange\ModelParameters\) while receiving the shared weights \(\ModelParameters\).

4 Non-IID (not independent and identically distributed): Samples may be dependent or drawn from different distributions; in a device fleet, user-specific label and feature distributions can make local updates conflict. Such distributions can degrade federated averaging relative to independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) baselines (Zhao et al. 2018). The observed failure determines whether to change local work, aggregation, or personalization rather than prescribing one remedy for every non-IID population.

Zhao, Yue, Meng Li, Liangzhen Lai, Naveen Suda, Damon Civin, and Vikas Chandra. 2018. “Federated Learning with Non-IID Data.” CoRR abs/1806.00582.

The choice of shared and personal state must fit the devices that execute it. Managed data centers can provision capacity and schedule training across dedicated resources; edge devices share limited power, memory, and cooling with foreground applications. Those local budgets determine which adaptation and coordination choices remain feasible.

11.3 Design Constraints

Fine-tuning a neural network on a smartphone is constrained by power and heat: a GPU training cluster may consume megawatts in a controlled facility, whereas a smartphone sustains only a few watts before throttling. Across the edge spectrum, memory ranges from a few gigabytes on phones down to hundreds of kilobytes on microcontrollers, sustained power ranges from a few watts down to milliwatts, and network connectivity may be intermittent or entirely absent. On-device learning must therefore use compute, memory, and data sparingly, and when a local model operates in a safety-critical loop, these resource limits become life-safety engineering problems.

Lighthouse 11.1: Archetype C (Federated MobileNet): The edge power wall
These constraints run through Archetype C (Federated MobileNet), where learning happens on phone-class devices whose power budget spans roughly 2–10 W, a few watts sustained with brief bursts toward 10 W, and which must share that budget with the user’s foreground work. Privacy and policy constraints often forbid moving raw examples off the device, and even where transfer is permitted, a mobile uplink and battery rarely afford shipping raw data to the cloud. Data locality is therefore both a policy requirement and a bandwidth and energy constraint. Quantized training, sparse updates, and federated coordination are the strategies that let Archetype C learn within that envelope. Held at that burst ceiling, an idealized 15 Wh battery would be exhausted in about 1.5 hours, and thermal throttling would end the workload sooner. Microcontroller-class sensors with hundreds of kilobytes of memory and milliwatt budgets sit at an extreme below Archetype C, and this chapter treats them as such.

The constraints on parameters, operations, and data interact. A larger batch can improve hardware utilization while exceeding memory capacity; a smaller trainable state can fit memory while extracting too little signal from local data. Compression provides one way to reach a feasible starting point. Quantization shrinks bytes per weight, pruning removes selected parameters, and knowledge distillation transfers behavior into a smaller architecture. Once the base model fits, local learning must also accommodate the state and work needed to adapt it.

On-device learning adds training state and work to the inference workload. A full-model update requires forward propagation, gradient computation through backpropagation, and weight updates. Memory grows because the update must hold gradients, optimizer state, and the activations saved for the backward pass. Arithmetic grows because the backward pass computes gradients with respect to both activations and weights, up to roughly twice the work of the forward pass. The size of each increase depends on the model, batch, precision, and execution schedule. Compression and selective adaptation address different parts of that pressure, so feasibility requires accounting for the resulting working set rather than applying a universal multiplier.

The relevant budget is the capacity available to the learning task. Centralized training can reserve resources and assemble datasets under a common management policy. A device must fit an update around the operating system, foreground applications, and locally available examples. Total installed memory or peak accelerator throughput therefore overstates what background learning can use.

These constraints define eligibility for a federated round. Model footprint and trainable state determine which device tiers can participate. Sparse, nonuniform data determines how much signal each local update can extract. Limited compute determines when training can run at all. A policy that exceeds the budget on weaker devices changes the population represented in the aggregate, even if it improves throughput on stronger devices.

11.3.1 Quantifying training overhead on edge devices

A compact 10M-parameter model whose 40 MB of FP32 weights fit comfortably inside a smartphone’s background-memory allowance can balloon to 360 MB as soon as backpropagation adds gradients, optimizer state, and saved activations. That total exceeds the background memory ceiling and crashes the process on its first backward pass. Training changes memory footprint, memory bandwidth, and hardware utilization through additional live state and backward operations. The illustrative increases in table 2 count different things from row to row, so each multiplier carries its own denominator. The 4–12× memory range compares a training working set with the inference weights alone, leaving inference activations and workspace out of the denominator. The 2–3\(\times\) compute range compares one update (forward plus a backward pass of one to two times the forward work) with one inference. The 10–50\(\times\) energy range compares a converged adaptation, which takes many gradient steps, with a single inference, so it is not a per-sample multiplier.

The memory row decomposes into four arrays (figure 3). Panel (a) starts with 40 MB of FP32 weights for a 10M-parameter model and adds 40 MB of gradients and 80 MB of Adaptive Moment Estimation (Adam) state for its two moment arrays. Adding 200 MB of assumed activation state brings the total to 360 MB, a 9× amplification over the weights. The same accounting spans the table’s range. Weights, gradients, and Adam’s two moments alone are four weight-sized arrays, the 4× floor reached when saved activations are negligible. Activations between five and eight times the weight bytes carry the total from 9× to 12×. Panel (b) holds the 9× proportions fixed across model scales from 1M to 100M parameters, so its constant ratio is an assumption rather than a finding. What it shows is the absolute cost of that ratio. The gap between weights and training stack grows from tens of megabytes at 1M parameters to gigabytes at 100M, so a model whose weights fit comfortably can still have a training stack that does not. Actual peak memory depends on optimizer choice, batch size, activation checkpointing, and how many layers are updated, and the 4–12× range describes these configurations rather than predicting any particular device.

↪ The bytes per parameter that weights, gradients, and optimizer state occupy in mixed-precision Adam training are developed in Numeric Precision and Training Memory.

Peak memory usage

One gradient update claims a quarter of the background-memory allowance.

Consider a smartphone keyboard that uses local gradient updates to adapt to a user’s vocabulary and typing patterns. In the illustrative device profile, an update requires 50 MB–100 MB for activations and optimizer state, against a background-memory allowance of 200 MB–300 MB. The actual allowance depends on the operating system, device, and foreground workload. At the selected operating point, the update consumes 25 percent of that allowance before other application state is counted. The design decision is how much learning can fit in the remaining budget while preserving responsive typing; the device’s total installed RAM does not answer it.

Whether an update stays inside that allowance depends on peak live residency over the step, because tensors are allocated and released dynamically as execution proceeds. The peak occurs when weights, saved activations, gradients, optimizer state, and temporary workspace coexist, which need not happen at the same operation in every implementation. For the 10M-parameter model of figure 3, 40 MB of FP32 weights belong to a 360 MB training working set, of which 200 MB is saved activations. Set against a smartphone’s 8 GB of installed RAM, that 360 MB working set looks modest, yet it exceeds even the top of the 200 MB–300 MB allowance that the operating system grants a background task after itself and the foreground applications. Activation checkpointing (also called gradient checkpointing) discards selected intermediate activations and recomputes them during the backward pass (Chen et al. 2016), the same trade that cuts activation memory in data-center training. It trades extra computation for lower activation storage, so its value depends on both the peak it removes and the additional time and energy the device can afford.

Chen, Tianqi, Bing Xu, Chiyuan Zhang, and Carlos Guestrin. 2016. “Training Deep Nets with Sublinear Memory Cost.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1604.06174.

↩︎ Activation checkpointing, which recomputes selected activations in the backward pass to cut activation memory at the cost of extra FLOPs, is established in Compute-phase forward and backward passes.

That allowance, not installed RAM, is the ceiling for a working-memory budget that every adaptation strategy in this chapter must meet (figure 4). The runtime stages frozen weights from nonvolatile flash into volatile working memory and reserves headroom below the physical device ceiling \(\DeviceMemoryCapacity\) for runtime and application state. Within the working-memory envelope \(\AdaptationPeakMemory\), four quantities contribute: let \(\ResidentWeightMemory\) be the backbone weights that must reside in working memory, \(\TrainableStateMemory\) the trainable parameters plus their gradients and optimizer state, \(\ActivationMemory(\TimeInstant)\) the saved activation state, and \(\OperatorWorkspaceMemory(\TimeInstant)\) operator workspace. With all terms counted in bytes and disjoint, the required working memory is

\[ \AdaptationPeakMemory= \ResidentWeightMemory+ \TrainableStateMemory+ \max_t\left[\ActivationMemory(\TimeInstant) + \OperatorWorkspaceMemory(\TimeInstant)\right]. \tag{1}\]

Admission then requires \(\AdaptationPeakMemory+ \ReservedDeviceMemory\le \DeviceMemoryCapacity\), where \(\ReservedDeviceMemory\) holds the runtime, the operating system, and the foreground application; for a background task, \(\DeviceMemoryCapacity- \ReservedDeviceMemory\) is the allowance just described. Weights stored in nonvolatile memory still consume deployment storage, and any staging buffers belong in \(\OperatorWorkspaceMemory(\TimeInstant)\). The maximum must follow the actual execution schedule because activation and workspace peaks need not coincide. Each term is a separate lever, and the adaptation strategies of section 11.4 differ in which term they shrink. Freezing weights removes their gradients and optimizer state from \(\TrainableStateMemory\), but it does not make the frozen base or every backward dependency disappear.

An inference optimization therefore needs a second feasibility check before it is used for adaptation. Reducing stored weights may leave activations or optimizer state as the dominant cost. Selecting which parameters to train changes that accounting, linking the memory analysis to the adaptation choices that follow.

Checkpoint 11.1: On-device training constraints

Verify understanding of how training amplifies edge constraints:

11.3.2 Model constraints

A model that fits comfortably on a smartphone can fail before a single backward pass on a smaller edge tier. MobileNetV2 requires approximately 14 MB of storage in its standard configuration. That fits easily in smartphone RAM yet far exceeds the 256 KB of SRAM and 1 MB of flash storage on microcontrollers such as the Arduino Nano 33 BLE Sense.5 On such severely constrained platforms, even a single convolutional layer may exceed available RAM during training due to intermediate feature maps and gradient storage. Cloud-deployed models can span billions of parameters across multi-gigabyte memory budgets. Models intended for on-device learning must conform to tight limits on memory, storage, and compute, each tightened further by the factors of table 2.

5 Arduino Nano 33 BLE Sense: With 256 KB SRAM, roughly 65,536× smaller than a flagship smartphone’s 16 GB, a single \(224{\times}224{\times}3\) RGB image occupies ~151 KB and consumes roughly 60 percent of available memory; activation and gradient storage add several more live tensors, and optimizer state can push the training footprint far beyond the inference footprint. A convolutional neural network (CNN) layer can therefore exceed SRAM during backpropagation even when its weights fit. Quantization can reduce this footprint, but feasibility also depends on input size, saved activations, and which parameters are updated.

Training adds state whose size cannot be inferred from layer count alone. Backpropagation retains or recomputes intermediate activations, with storage determined by tensor shapes, precision, batch size, and the update schedule. The arithmetic grows quickly. A 10-layer convolutional model processing \(64{\times}64\) images saves feature maps of \(64{\times}64\) values per channel when its layers keep the input resolution. At FP32, every channel of every retained map then costs 16,384 bytes, about 16 KB. Ten layers of even a few channels each therefore outgrow the 256 KB of microcontroller SRAM before a single gradient or optimizer value is stored. The relevant comparison is that peak against the memory available to learning after runtime and application reserves, which is why the techniques later in this chapter attack saved activations as well as trainable parameters.

Model complexity also affects runtime energy consumption and thermal limits. In smartwatches or battery-powered wearables, sustained training can deplete the energy allowance or trigger thermal throttling even when its tensors fit in memory. Arithmetic precision alone does not determine feasibility; the update duration and measured power determine its energy cost. Benchmarks such as MLPerf Tiny provide small quantized inference targets for constrained devices (Banbury et al. 2021). On-device adaptation techniques address the additional training cost by freezing most parameters, reducing activation storage, or selecting sparse update sets (Cai et al. 2020; Kwon et al. 2024).

Banbury, Colby, Vijay Janapa Reddi, Peter Torelli, Jeremy Holleman, Nat Jeffries, Csaba Kiraly, Pietro Montino, et al. 2021. “MLPerf Tiny Benchmark.” arXiv Preprint.

Battery and thermal constraints also determine when learning can run. The illustrative smartphone profile uses a sustained processing envelope of 2–3 W and brief bursts of 5–10 W. An update that fits during an idle interval may exceed the available headroom when foreground work starts. Charging supplies energy, but the runtime must still monitor heat and contention, reducing the update duty cycle or suspending learning when limits are approached. An overnight idle-and-charging policy provides one candidate window; measured device conditions determine whether that window remains usable.

These constraints make adaptation feasibility part of architecture selection. When the base model exceeds device capacity, partitioning, quantization, offloading, or a smaller architecture can change the deployment footprint, each with its own execution cost. INT8 or INT4 weights often decide whether the base model fits at all, and 1-bit weights cut weight bytes 16\(\times\) relative to FP16 (32\(\times\) relative to FP32) before scale metadata and activations are counted. Fitting the forward path is the starting point; quantization support and selective-update costs must then be established for the training path.

11.3.3 Data constraints

Fitting the model and its update state into memory solves only the capacity side of the problem, because the available local data still sets how much a fitted update can actually learn. A fitness tracker that records thousands of accelerometer readings across a workout may receive only a single user-supplied activity label, or it may record from a sensor whose calibration has drifted. Unlike the curated datasets used in cloud training, edge data is locally collected, temporally sparse, and often unlabeled. Because sampling frequency, windowing, and temporal correlation can collapse an entire session of correlated sensor readings into a single independent observation, the learning policy must budget for usable supervision rather than raw stored bytes.

On-device data is also frequently non-i.i.d. (Zhao et al. 2018). A voice assistant deployed across households encounters variation in accents, languages, speaking styles, and command patterns. Smartphone keyboards observe individual typing patterns, autocorrect preferences, and multilingual usage. Centralized systems also encounter such variation, but a federated coordinator cannot freely shuffle raw examples across clients. Local updates therefore reflect different data distributions, complicating the balance between shared generalization and personalization.

Label scarcity compounds the distribution problem. Most edge-collected data is unlabeled by default, requiring systems to learn from weak or implicit supervision signals. A smartphone camera may capture thousands of images throughout the day, but only a few are associated with meaningful user actions (tagging, favoriting, or sharing) that could serve as implicit labels. In many applications, including anomaly detection in sensor data and gesture recognition adaptation, explicit labels may be entirely unavailable, making traditional supervised learning infeasible without alternative methods for weak supervision or unsupervised adaptation.

Data quality introduces further challenges. Environmental sensors or automotive ECUs can experience calibration changes, interference, or mechanical wear that corrupt or shift input signals. An adaptation loop may learn from those changes as if they represented useful new behavior. Local input checks and validated outcome signals must distinguish sensor faults from changes the model should learn, especially when raw observations are unavailable for central inspection.

Privacy and security requirements can further restrict which examples may be retained or shared. Keeping health data, personal communications, or behavioral traces on the device narrows raw-data exposure, but local storage and transmitted updates remain sensitive. The learning design must specify access, retention, and permitted disclosures alongside the data needed for adaptation and validation.

11.3.4 Compute constraints

Even when a compact model and validated local examples are in place, the processor that runs inference may not be able to train them: a mobile neural processing unit (NPU) advertised at tens of TOPS for INT8 inference can lack backward-pass or optimizer kernels entirely. An on-device update must then fall back to the CPU and run an order of magnitude slower and hotter than the chip’s inference rating suggests. Across the edge hardware spectrum, from microcontrollers like the STM32F4 and ESP32, through mobile processors with dedicated AI accelerators (Apple Neural Engine, Qualcomm Hexagon, Google Tensor), to high-capability edge modules, inference ratings measure forward-pass throughput on fixed weights. Training workloads add backward and optimizer operations with very different execution paths.

Because an inference rating does not measure this backward path, feasibility depends on tracing where each update operator actually executes. Backpropagation reads saved or recomputed activations, produces gradients, and updates parameters and optimizer state. These operations add memory traffic, with the amount determined by the trainable subset and execution schedule. A specialized accelerator can expand the inference budget without executing any backward operator. The feasibility test is therefore the actual update path. It identifies which processor runs each backward and optimizer operator, including any fallback to the CPU or GPU, and measures time, memory, and energy for that path under foreground load. Each device class that follows gets this update-path test, applied to its own memory, operators, and power, rather than a verdict read from its inference rating.

At the most constrained end, devices such as the STM32F46 or ESP32-S3 microcontrollers offer only a few hundred kilobytes of SRAM and limited compute and power budgets (Warden and Situnayake 2020). Although these families include single-precision floating-point support, practical ML deployments commonly rely on quantized or fixed-point kernels for efficiency. Libraries like CMSIS-NN (Lai et al. 2018) provide optimized neural network kernels for Arm Cortex-M processors, achieving 4.6\(\times\) runtime improvement through fixed-point arithmetic and single instruction, multiple data optimizations. These severe limitations preclude conventional deep learning libraries and require models designed for quantized arithmetic and minimal runtime memory allocation. Quantization-aware training7 can prepare models for efficient quantized inference. Executing local updates requires a separately supported training path, potentially with selective updates, quantized gradients, or a compact floating-point working set.

6 STM32F4 microcontroller: With 192 KB SRAM, a 168 MHz clock speed, and a single-precision floating-point unit, the main constraint is not the absence of floating point but the tiny memory and energy envelope. A dense layer with 1,000 input features and 10 hidden units requires approximately 40 KB for weights and biases in FP32, consuming about 20 percent of total SRAM before accounting for activations or gradients. Local updates must therefore fit around the device’s primary workload and power budget, and INT8 or fixed-point kernels remain preferable when accuracy allows.

Warden, Pete, and Daniel Situnayake. 2020. TinyML: Machine Learning with TensorFlow Lite on Arduino and Ultra-Low-Power Microcontrollers. O’Reilly Media.
Lai, Liangzhen, Naveen Suda, and Vikas Chandra. 2018. “CMSIS-NN: Efficient Neural Network Kernels for Arm Cortex-M CPUs.” ArXiv Preprint abs/1801.06601.

7 Quantization-aware training (QAT): Simulates quantization during training to adapt the model to low-precision inference (Jacob et al. 2018; Krishnamoorthi 2018). Fake-quantization operations commonly retain floating-point training tensors, so QAT does not itself imply integer backward kernels or a smaller training-state budget. On-device adaptation must separately establish supported update arithmetic, state storage, and measured energy costs.

Jacob, Benoit, Skirmantas Kligys, Bo Chen, Menglong Zhu, Matthew Tang, Andrew Howard, Hartwig Adam, and Dmitry Kalenichenko. 2018. “Quantization and Training of Neural Networks for Efficient Integer-Arithmetic-Only Inference.” 2018 IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition, 2704–13. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2018.00286.
Krishnamoorthi, Raghuraman. 2018. “Quantizing Deep Convolutional Networks for Efficient Inference: A Whitepaper.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1806.08342 abs/1806.08342.

8 SGD (stochastic gradient descent): Plain SGD keeps parameters and gradients, two parameter-sized arrays where Adam keeps four, so its lower state cost must still be weighed against the updates needed to reach the quality target.

Microcontroller memory rules out training configurations whose live tensors exceed SRAM, rather than every convolutional model. Feasibility depends on tensor shapes, update scope, numerical representation, and the compiled execution schedule. Plain stochastic gradient descent (SGD) reduces optimizer state compared with methods that retain additional moment arrays.8 Non-neural adaptation, such as \(\ClusterCount\)-means clustering, offers a different way to update local centroids for sensor analysis. The method and its arithmetic must still fit the available memory and produce a validated improvement; neither a simple optimizer nor integer execution establishes that result alone.

Moving up the computational hierarchy, mobile-class hardware represents improvement but still operates under severe constraints. Platforms including the Qualcomm Snapdragon, Apple Neural Engine,9 and the Google Tensor system-on-chip (SoC) provide significantly more compute power than microcontrollers, often featuring dedicated AI accelerators and optimized support for 8-bit or mixed-precision matrix operations. These accelerators offer dedicated matrix multiplication units, on-chip memory hierarchies, and power management features specifically designed for neural network inference, with varying support for local training workloads. While these platforms can support more sophisticated training routines, including full backpropagation over compact models, they still fall far short of the computational throughput and memory bandwidth available in centralized data centers. Training a lightweight transformer on a smartphone, for instance, is technically feasible but must be tightly bounded in both time and energy to avoid degrading the user experience.

9 Apple Neural Engine: Apple’s dedicated neural accelerator, first shipped in the A11 Bionic in 2017; its rating growth through the A17 Pro is tracked in figure 5. Which of an update’s operators actually reach it, rather than falling back to the CPU or GPU, is the runtime question worked through in section 11.3.4.3.

Hardware capability contributes to on-device adaptation feasibility alongside the algorithm and runtime. The mobile accelerator scaling tracked in figure 5 shows Apple’s stated rating climbing from under 1 TOPS on the A11 Bionic to nearly 35 TOPS on the A17 Pro, a step in 2023 that may partly reflect a change in rating precision rather than in the hardware alone. These peak ratings concern forward inference. A training budget must also account for backward operators and the runtime that dispatches them.

Systems Perspective 11.1: The silicon dividend of a dedicated NPU
The gap between running a model on a generic mobile CPU and on a dedicated NPU is best understood as a “silicon dividend” rather than a marginal speedup. Published NPU profiles vary by silicon generation and workload, so this callout uses explicit scenario assumptions rather than a benchmark: a 20× latency improvement and a 60× energy-efficiency improvement relative to the modeled MobileNetV2 baseline (1.883 ms per inference on the CPU).

The dividend belongs to the operators the NPU actually executes. An update adds backward and optimizer work that costs roughly one to two more forward passes, because the backward pass computes gradients with respect to both activations and weights. If the runtime cannot place those operators on the NPU, they fall back to the CPU. The forward pass may still collect its 20× dividend, but CPU backward work and transfers between processors can dominate the complete update. The resulting speed and energy depend on that mixed execution path. An inference-dominated product and an adaptation-dominated one can therefore see very different returns from the same silicon, and only the traced update path says which case a deployment is in.

Published benchmark results from MLPerf Tiny and vendor data illustrate the range of edge inference costs in figure 6. The plotted tiers span roughly 100\(\times\) in energy per inference. Dedicated neural processors such as the Syntiant Core 2 and STM32N6 NPU occupy the sub-millijoule MCU and application-specific integrated circuit (ASIC) band. They use 30 to 444 \(\Glyph{\mu}\)J per inference across keyword spotting, visual wake words, and small-image classification, and keyword spotting finishes in under 5 ms. Google Coral appears near 4.8 mJ, and the Jetson AGX Orin appears near 15 mJ for ResNet-50. The roughly 100\(\times\) gap mixes different workloads with different hardware, so it is not a matched-task efficiency comparison. Energy per inference does not determine battery lifetime on its own, because inference frequency, sleep power, sensing, radio use, and usable battery capacity also enter that budget. For a learning device the figure sets the scale against which an update must be judged. One evaluation costs microjoules to millijoules, while each second of training inside the 2–3 W sustained envelope spends joules, more than a thousand keyword-spotting evaluations at the plotted 30 to 160 \(\Glyph{\mu}\)J. An update can therefore be a substantial energy cost, but its share of the device budget depends on how often training and inference run. Both paths need measurement over the same operating period.

These computational limitations become especially acute in real-time or battery-operated systems, where the foreground latency budgets of section 11.2.1 bound the interference local training may introduce without requiring training to finish within an inference deadline. Training also has different memory access patterns from inference. Gradient computation, activation storage, and optimizer updates add memory traffic whose magnitude depends on which parameters train and which intermediate states are retained. These transfers can create bottlenecks that pure computational metrics do not capture. Hardware designs can address these challenges with specialized support, but a particular device must expose that support through its training runtime. Mixed-precision datapaths may execute different formats in forward and backward kernels when those kernels exist. Sparse-update savings require an execution path that can actually skip the selected work; zero gradients alone do not establish reduced hardware cost (section 11.4.4). Near-memory compute architectures10 reduce data movement costs by performing gradient updates directly adjacent to weight storage, addressing the memory bandwidth bottleneck. Most edge accelerators, however, remain optimized for inference, which leaves the backward and optimizer operators of local adaptation as an open hardware-software co-design problem.

10 Near-memory computing: Places processing elements adjacent to or within memory arrays to cut the data movement that often dominates arithmetic energy (Horowitz 2014; Kwon and Rhu 2018), which could make write-heavy on-device backpropagation more practical.

Horowitz, Mark. 2014. “1.1 Computing’s Energy Problem (and What We Can Do about It).” 2014 IEEE International Solid-State Circuits Conference Digest of Technical Papers (ISSCC), 10–14. https://doi.org/10.1109/isscc.2014.6757323.
Kwon, Youngeun, and Minsoo Rhu. 2018. “TensorDIMM: A Practical Near-Memory Processing Architecture for Embeddings and Tensor Operations in Deep Learning.” Proceedings of the 52nd Annual IEEE/ACM International Symposium on Microarchitecture, 740–53. https://doi.org/10.1145/3352460.3358284.

The mobile memory wall

Rising NPU ratings tempt designers to read device capability from TOPS, but every byte a local update moves crosses the same memory interface as inference traffic, and an update moves far more bytes than an inference. Counting them for the 10M-parameter model of figure 3, under the simplest assumption that each tensor crosses DRAM once per use with no cache reuse, shows how far. The forward pass reads the weights and writes the saved activations, and the backward pass rereads the activations and weights and writes the gradients. An Adam step then reads the weights, gradients, and both moments and writes the weights and moments back. One update step moves about 800 MB, 20× the weight bytes a single inference reads. At the 51 GB/s peak bandwidth of an A17 Pro, that traffic alone needs about 16 ms per step before any arithmetic is counted. Mobile language-model decode, the case already met as the autoregressive bottleneck (principle 18), shows the same ceiling at its starkest, since decode pressure falls on memory and interconnect rather than peak arithmetic throughput (Ma and Patterson 2026). The interface itself is narrow by data-center standards. Data-center HBM3 on an H100 provides 3,350 GB/s of peak unidirectional DRAM memory bandwidth. Flagship mobile systems sit near 51 GB/s (A17 Pro, LPDDR5) to 77 GB/s (Snapdragon 8 Gen 3, LPDDR5X), a gap of roughly 44–65×.

Ma, Xiaoyu, and David Patterson. 2026. Challenges and Research Directions for Large Language Model Inference Hardware. No. 5. Vol. 59. https://doi.org/10.1109/mc.2026.3652916.

↩︎ The memory wall, the widening gap between arithmetic throughput and the memory bandwidth that feeds it, is established in The Memory Wall.

Data-center high-bandwidth memory (HBM) outruns flagship mobile memory bandwidth by about 44 times.

A bandwidth gap of this size gives a corresponding gap in a bandwidth-only time ceiling only when both devices stream the same bytes and sustain the same fraction of their peak bandwidth. Precision, batching, cache reuse, and runtime overhead all move the measured ratio. Low-precision weights attack that ceiling when weight traffic is what binds. Storing FP32 weights at 4 bits, for example, reduces weight bytes by 8\(\times\) and reduces that component of traffic by the same factor if the runtime consumes the compressed representation efficiently. It does not increase physical bandwidth or remove cache-state traffic and decoding overhead, and the precision choice must satisfy both quality and measured latency requirements. For an on-device update the relief is smaller still. In the count above, the three weight reads are only about 15 percent of the step’s traffic, and a quantized base leaves the saved activations, gradients, and optimizer state that make up the rest untouched.

Systems Perspective 11.2: Why bandwidth, not TOPS, is the binding constraint
Small-batch decode is the familiar memory-bound case. Each token rereads weights too large to stay in cache, so the data term \((\DataVolume / \Bandwidth)\) of the single-machine iron law sets its time, and sustained bandwidth and bytes per token predict it better than peak TOPS. The roofline recap in The roofline bound works that decode example. Local adaptation needs the same comparison applied to its own forward, backward, and optimizer traffic, counted per update step rather than per token, where the count above puts it at about 800 MB.

Memory hierarchy optimization

Device memory spans more than four orders of magnitude, phone to microcontroller.

Han, Song, Jeff Pool, John Tran, and William J. Dally. 2015. “Learning Both Weights and Connections for Efficient Neural Networks.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 28 (NeurIPS 2015), 1135–43.
Hinton, Geoffrey, Oriol Vinyals, and Jeff Dean. 2015. “Distilling the Knowledge in a Neural Network.” arXiv Preprint.

Capacity is the question equation 1 already answers. Quantization, pruning, and distillation can each shrink the resident and trainable terms of that working set (Jacob et al. 2018; Han et al. 2015; Hinton et al. 2015), but their gains are workload-specific and do not combine automatically, so they must be validated together against the same budget. Once the working set fits, capacity says nothing about the second question, which is how much traffic the update drives through the hierarchy and how much of that traffic the caches can absorb. Operator tiling and memory layout aim to reuse active tensor blocks before eviction, while batch size controls activation storage and available parallelism. Gradient accumulation can limit the microbatch working set, but it also retains accumulation state and adds accesses, so its effect on traffic must be measured.

Training adds gradient and optimizer traffic to the tensor reads and writes already required by inference. When these transfers saturate memory bandwidth, additional arithmetic capacity cannot shorten the update. The activation checkpointing introduced with peak memory usage shows why capacity and traffic must be counted separately. Recomputing activations lowers the peak footprint while adding forward work and its memory reads, so capacity can improve as traffic and energy rise. Mixed-precision training changes bytes per stored tensor, but numerical stability and supported backward kernels determine whether the smaller representation is useful. Capacity, traffic, and update time therefore need separate measurements.

Mobile AI accelerator optimization

Accelerator choice depends on the training primitives that the device’s software stack exposes. Apple Neural Engine, Qualcomm Hexagon DSP, and Google Tensor devices have different compiler and runtime contracts, so the same update can follow different paths on each. A useful execution plan identifies where feature extraction, head or adapter training, and optimizer updates run. A dedicated engine may accelerate the frozen forward path while another processor handles learning, but transfers and duplicate representations then enter the cost. The supported operators and data movement must be established for the actual device and runtime version.

A local update may therefore execute on a CPU or GPU even when inference uses the dedicated neural accelerator. On a real device, the update-path test that opened the discussion of compute constraints becomes a short procedure.

  1. List the update’s operators in execution order: the forward pass, each backward operator that the trainable subset requires, and the optimizer step.
  2. For each operator, record the processor the runtime actually places it on, noting every fallback from the neural engine to the CPU or GPU.
  3. At each change of processor, add the transfer and any duplicate tensor representation to the update’s memory and traffic accounting.
  4. Measure time, peak memory, and energy for the complete traced path while a representative foreground workload runs.
  5. Feed those measurements into the peak-memory budget of equation 1 and the admission test of equation 2 that follows, which together decide whether the update may run.

Mixed-precision datapaths, sparse-update support, and near-memory compute can reduce those costs, but none of them is a prerequisite for training. What matters is whether the available runtime can execute the required operators within the device envelope. Architecture selection therefore shapes update precision, parameter selection, communication, and thermal policy together.

11.3.5 Energy, heat, and background admission

An update path that fits in memory and runs on supported kernels still fails if 30 minutes of background backpropagation noticeably drains the battery, warms the handset in the user’s pocket, or delays foreground touch responses. Because energy draw, thermal dissipation, and shared-processor contention reach the user directly, a mobile power budget constrains both the model complexity a device can train and the schedule on which background learning is admitted.

Napkin Math 11.1: Battery drain: The cost of edge learning
Problem: A team is designing a background fine-tuning job for a personalized voice assistant on a smartphone. Run unconstrained, the training job draws 4.5 W, above the 2–3 W sustained envelope given earlier, and takes 30 minutes to complete. If the phone has a 15 Wh battery, how much of the user’s battery will this “invisible” update consume?

Math:

  1. Total energy: 4.5 W \(\times\) 0.5 h = 2.25 Wh.
  2. Battery impact: 2.25 Wh/15 Wh = 15 percent.

Systems insight: Consuming 15 percent of a user’s battery for one background task makes update frequency a deployment decision. That energy cannot be translated into a fixed amount of screen-on time without specifying the foreground workload. A policy of opportunistic scheduling admits learning when device conditions permit it; a federated policy might require charging, Wi-Fi, and thermal headroom. Charging supplies energy but does not remove heat or foreground contention, so those constraints still need separate checks.

This battery calculation is the local edge-learning version of a broader energy constraint. Training must satisfy the user’s energy budget in addition to the model’s accuracy target. A production design therefore has to account for energy at scheduling time, compare local computation against communication cost, and defer learning when the user’s device cannot absorb the update.

That decision can be expressed as a background-work admission condition. Let \(\AdaptationWindowEnergy\) be the predicted energy for one adaptation window, \(\AdaptationEnergyAllowance\) the energy the policy permits before the next charging opportunity, \(\DevicePower\) the current sustained power, \(\AdaptationPowerIncrement\) the adaptation increment, and \(\ThermalPowerLimit\) the thermally sustainable limit. Let \(\ForegroundLatency\) be measured foreground latency, \(\AdaptationLatencyIncrease\) the worst-case latency increase under concurrent adaptation, and \(\CompletionDeadline\) the foreground deadline. The runtime admits an update only if

\[ \AdaptationWindowEnergy\le \AdaptationEnergyAllowance\quad \land \quad \DevicePower+ \AdaptationPowerIncrement\le \ThermalPowerLimit\quad \land \quad \ForegroundLatency+ \AdaptationLatencyIncrease\le \CompletionDeadline. \tag{2}\]

Charging can enlarge the energy allowance, but it does not waive the thermal or foreground-latency tests. The quantities must come from device- and workload-specific profiling, and the runtime must revoke admission when the measured envelope changes. Shorter update windows, a lower duty cycle, or a smaller trainable parameter set can make a rejected update feasible; allowing background learning to consume the foreground deadline cannot.

The same test also decides who teaches a shared model. Passing it correlates with hardware class, charging habits, and connectivity, so devices that are old, rarely charged, or thermally tight contribute few updates. As a result, the population a federated model learns from is the admitted population rather than the deployed one (principle 20). The admission policy that protects each device is therefore also a sampling policy, and section 11.7.1 measures the skew it creates.

Example 11.1: On-device keyboard learning
Scenario: Consider a mobile keyboard that trains its next-word predictor locally and contributes updates to a shared model through federated averaging (McMahan et al. 2017).

Diagnosis: In the keyboard profile of section 11.3.1.1, one local update needs 50 MB–100 MB of working memory against a background allowance of 200 MB–300 MB. At the selected operating point, the update alone therefore claims 25 percent of the allowance before the keyboard’s own state is counted, and the resident weights in \(\AdaptationPeakMemory\) claim more. Exceeding the allowance risks process eviction mid-update. Energy is the second constraint. An unconstrained job like the one in notebook 11.1 would take 15 percent of the battery, more than a policy that protects the user’s charge is likely to grant as \(\AdaptationEnergyAllowance\) in equation 2 while the phone runs on battery. The pipeline therefore admits training only when the device is idle, charging, and on Wi-Fi, and it quantizes update vectors to shrink the transmitted payload \(\DataVolume\).

Systems lesson: A federated keyboard must satisfy all three terms of equation 2 (energy \(\AdaptationWindowEnergy\le \AdaptationEnergyAllowance\), sustained power under \(\ThermalPowerLimit\), and typing latency under its deadline), fit \(\AdaptationPeakMemory\) within its memory allowance, respect uplink bandwidth \(\Bandwidth\), and meet its declared privacy guarantees together. Differential privacy adds a specific release guarantee and its associated costs; it is not implied by keeping raw text local.

11.3.6 Matching edge constraints to adaptation pillars

Memory, data, compute, and admission constraints act on the same update window at once. Shrinking trainable parameters so an update fits in RAM still fails if local supervision is too sparse to guide the step or if intermittent connectivity drops the device from every federated round. The constraint analysis therefore yields three linked design decisions. The training amplification of table 2 motivates measuring the update footprint against equation 2 and choosing which parameters to adapt. Information scarcity, including limited local datasets, non-IID distributions, restrictions on data sharing, and minimal supervision, motivates data efficiency methods that reuse available evidence. When shared learning is useful, device heterogeneity and intermittent availability motivate federated coordination with explicit validation and privacy controls. Each pillar answers a different constraint within the same deployment budget and brings its own techniques to that answer (table 3).

Self-Check: Question

  1. A mobile engineering team observes that fine-tuning a 10M parameter vision model on a smartphone with 8 GB of RAM causes the background training process to be killed by the operating system’s out-of-memory (OOM) killer during backpropagation. Why does training memory demand spike so severely compared to inference for the exact same model?

    1. The base model weights dynamically expand by \(100\times\) during the forward pass due to weight unpacking.
    2. Floating-point units on mobile SoCs automatically convert all INT8 weights to FP64 double precision during training.
    3. Training adds gradients, optimizer state, and saved backward activations to the weights; the simultaneous peak can exceed the app’s budget even when inference fits.
    4. The mobile operating system creates a separate virtual machine sandbox for every individual neuron in the neural network.

  2. A quantized 7B-parameter language model achieves 12 tokens/sec on a mobile SoC advertised at 45 peak TOPS. Profiling shows that low-batch decode saturates available DRAM bandwidth while arithmetic units wait. Which hardware resource is the binding constraint?

    1. Insufficient CPU integer arithmetic capacity for calculating cross-entropy loss.
    2. Thermal throttling of the wireless 5G modem during token generation.
    3. Flash storage read latency when loading the token tokenizer vocabulary.
    4. Mobile LPDDR5/LPDDR5X memory bandwidth (\(51\text{–}77\text{ GB/s}\)), which is roughly \(44\text{–}65\times\) lower than data-center HBM3, causing the memory streaming term \((D_{\text{vol}} / \text{BW})\) to dominate execution time per token.

  3. A proposed background personalization task on a smartphone draws \(4.5\text{ W}\) of sustained SoC power and requires 30 minutes to complete. Calculate the total energy consumed (in \(\text{Wh}\)) and the percentage drain on an idealized \(15\text{ Wh}\) smartphone battery. Explain why this result mandates opportunistic scheduling.

  4. True or False: Gradient checkpointing reduces the total electrical energy consumed by an on-device training workload because it reduces peak SRAM/DRAM memory allocation.

  5. Consider the end-to-end memory lifecycle of a single gradient update during on-device backpropagation for a convolutional layer. Order the following operational stages from first to last in execution sequence:

  1. Discard intermediate activations and compute parameter updates in optimizer state
  2. Forward pass computes and caches layer activations
  3. Fetch batch inputs into SRAM buffer
  4. Loss evaluation and backward pass calculates layer gradients using cached activations

See Answers →

11.4 Model Adaptation

The 10M-parameter model of figure 3 is an inference candidate for a smartwatch with 500 MB of total system memory, because its FP32 weights occupy 40 MB. Training every parameter would need the full 360 MB stack, most of the watch’s memory before its operating system and foreground application take their share. The weight footprint alone does not establish inference fit; activation buffers, runtime workspace, and foreground reserve must also fit. Full-model training leaves much less room for that remaining state. Freezing parameters removes their gradients and optimizer state but not the storage or execution cost of the frozen network. The system therefore trains only a compact, strategically placed parameter set and, where it can, also shrinks the activations that the backward pass must keep. Resource-efficient model adaptation then determines both the local work a device can complete and the shared update format the fleet can aggregate.

The engineering choice balances adaptation expressivity against resource consumption. Full fine-tuning exposes all parameters to learning and can fit sufficiently small models; retaining a fixed model can be adequate when local context already supplies the needed personalization. Between these choices, selective updates offer smaller training state. The device resource envelope determines which candidates fit: a wearable-class device with 1 MB RAM has a different budget from a smartphone with 8 GB. The local variation then determines how much trainable capacity is useful. Bias updates may capture calibration changes, while shifts requiring new features need a richer update space. Runtime support, inference cost, shared-update compatibility, and rollback determine whether the candidate can enter the deployed system.

The selection of adaptation techniques follows this matching logic, from weight freezing (section 11.4.2) to structured and sparse updates (section 11.4.4). Their order of presentation is not a universal ranking of cost or expressivity; placement, update size, and runtime support determine those properties. Building on the resource accounting in section 11.3, the target is the smallest update space that captures the local variation: preserve what works globally, adapt what matters locally. Weight freezing restricts training to selected existing parameters, such as bias terms or final layers, whereas structured updates add low-rank or residual components to a frozen base and sparse updates select existing parameters according to their adaptation value and measured cost. Each choice must preserve enough useful capacity while fitting the device’s update budget.

11.4.1 Transfer as the premise

All three adaptation families work only because the device does not learn its representation from scratch. Training a feature extractor from random initialization on a few dozen noisy phone interactions would overfit immediately and exhaust the energy allowance before learning anything generalizable. Instead, every local update that fits an edge budget relies on knowledge transfer, in which a cloud-pretrained model supplies representations useful for a new task or domain. Reusing those representations reduces what the device must learn from local examples, and the benefit depends on what the tasks share. A model pretrained in the cloud can adapt with limited local updates when its representations retain the distinctions the new task requires. If a new input modality or objective requires features the base model does not provide, a small local update may be insufficient.

Transfer is therefore the premise of the adaptation methods that follow. When pretrained representations carry the distinctions a personal typing vocabulary, an individual gait, or a drifting sensor needs, bias-only updates, adapters, and head-only training can learn a small correction while freezing most of the network. When that premise fails, head-only adaptation cannot recover distinctions absent from its fixed features. Earlier-layer adapters or broader updates may learn them, but their added state, computation, and data requirements must fit the device budget.

11.4.2 Weight freezing

When pretrained features already capture the structure of the task, the most direct way to shrink \(\TrainableStateMemory\) in equation 1 is to freeze the majority of a model’s parameters and adapt only a minimal subset. Bias-only adaptation holds all weights fixed and updates only the bias terms (scalar offsets applied after linear or convolutional layers). The constraint reduces trainable parameters by 100–1000\(\times\), removes the weight gradients and their optimizer state from \(\TrainableStateMemory\), and helps mitigate overfitting when training data is sparse or noisy.

Consider a standard neural network layer: \[ \LayerOutputVector= \WeightMatrix\LayerInputVector+ \BiasVector\] where \(\WeightMatrix\in \mathbb{R}^{\WeightMatrixRowCount\times \WeightMatrixColumnCount}\) is the weight matrix, \(\BiasVector\in \mathbb{R}^\WeightMatrixRowCount\) is the bias vector, and \(\LayerInputVector\in \mathbb{R}^\WeightMatrixColumnCount\) is the input. In full training, gradients are computed and applied to both \(\WeightMatrix\) and \(\BiasVector\). In bias-only adaptation, the system freezes the weight tensor (\(\FiniteChange\WeightMatrix= \ZeroMatrix\)) and computes gradients exclusively for the bias: \[ \WeightMatrix\leftarrow \WeightMatrix, \quad \BiasVector\leftarrow \BiasVector- \LearningRate\frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \BiasVector} \]

This reduces the number of stored gradients and optimizer states, enabling training to proceed under memory-constrained conditions. The smaller trainable state helps only if the device also supports the arithmetic and activation schedule required by the update. In the bias-only setup of listing 1, all convolutional and fully connected weights remain frozen, and only the bias terms adapt to local data.

This pattern restricts optimizer updates to bias terms. What it saves, and what it does not, follows from the two derivatives of the layer above.
