The weight gradient \(\partial \LossFunction/\partial \WeightMatrix= (\partial \LossFunction/\partial \LayerOutputVector)\,\LayerInputVector^T\) needs the layer’s saved input \(\LayerInputVector\), whereas the bias gradient \(\partial \LossFunction/\partial \BiasVector= \partial \LossFunction/\partial \LayerOutputVector\) needs only the gradient arriving from above. A frozen linear layer therefore no longer has to keep its input for its own sake. Reaching a bias in an earlier layer, however, still sends the gradient back through every later layer. Each nonlinearity or normalization on that path needs its own saved state (a ReLU needs at least the mask of which inputs were positive). Freezing parameters thus shrinks \(\TrainableStateMemory\) without eliminating backward computation or all saved activation state. This is valuable when adapting pretrained models to user-specific or device-local data where the core representations remain relevant but require calibration.

Tiny Transfer Learning (TinyTL) (Cai et al. 2020) targets the activation term of equation 1 as well as trainable state. It builds on the derivative argument above. Because frozen linear and convolutional layers drop their saved inputs, the remaining activation footprint comes from nonlinearities, normalization layers, and any trainable side-paths added to recover expressivity. Adding a standard residual adapter beside a frozen block would require saving that block’s full-resolution input feature map to compute the adapter’s weight gradients, undoing the memory saving. TinyTL avoids that trap with lite residual modules that downsample the input feature map and project it to a narrow channel width before applying trainable convolutions, so the backward pass caches only a small low-resolution tensor at each block alongside compact group-normalization and activation state.

Full fine-tuning (Panel A of figure 7) computes gradients for every intermediate layer weight and caches every block’s full input map, whereas TinyTL (Panel B) freezes the convolutional backbone (gray blocks) and confines parameter updates to the downsampled lite residual side-paths and bias vectors \(\BiasVector\) (blue blocks). The dashed backward-pass arrow spans the full network depth in both architectures. TinyTL’s memory reduction therefore stems not from truncating the backward pass, but from replacing full-resolution saved feature maps with the much smaller inputs of the lite residual modules.

The memory reduction is useful only when the frozen backbone remains expressive enough for the downstream task. The bias terms allow for minor but meaningful shifts in model behavior, particularly for personalization tasks. When domain shift is more significant, TinyTL can optionally incorporate small residual adapters to improve expressivity, all while preserving the system’s tight memory and energy profile.

TinyTL demonstrates that reducing saved activation state can matter more than reducing the number of trainable parameters alone (Cai et al. 2020). On its image-classification transfer benchmarks, TinyTL reports training-memory savings of up to 6.5× relative to fine-tuning the full network with little accuracy loss, so the adaptation working set can shrink to roughly 15 percent of the full fine-tuning footprint. The lite residual modules that recover adaptation capacity add only about 3.8 percent memory overhead in that study. Those savings depend on the selected feature extractor, input resolution, batch size, normalization, and residual-module configuration. A saving measured on those benchmarks says nothing about whether an arbitrary frozen backbone fits into microcontroller SRAM, and when a deployed model must learn richer feature combinations without custom downsampled side-paths, it needs general structured updates beside the frozen weights.

Cai, Han, Chuang Gan, Ligeng Zhu, and Song Han. 2020. “TinyTL: Reduce Activations, Not Trainable Parameters for Efficient on-Device Learning.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 33: 11285–97.

11.4.3 Structured parameter updates

Adapting a smartphone camera pipeline to a new low-light sensor profile or a mobile language model to a user’s technical vocabulary requires recombining features across channels, a change that scalar bias offsets \(\BiasVector\) cannot express while the weight matrices \(\WeightMatrix\) remain fixed. Unfreezing \(\WeightMatrix\) directly would restore the full gradient and optimizer arrays in \(\TrainableStateMemory\), so the system instead adds compact, structured trainable matrices beside the frozen backbone. Residual adaptation modules (Houlsby et al. 2019) and low-rank parameterizations (Hu et al. 2021) implement this choice in different ways. Both restrict parameter updates to the added components, but their training cost still includes the backward path through the surrounding network. The comparison with bias-only or full fine-tuning therefore requires both a quality target and a peak-memory measurement.

Houlsby, Neil, Andrei Giurgiu, Stanislaw Jastrzebski, Bruna Morrone, Quentin de Laroussilhe, Andrea Gesmundo, Mohammad Attariyan, and Sylvain Gelly. 2019. “Parameter-Efficient Transfer Learning for NLP.” Proceedings of the 36th International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 97: 2790–99.

Adapter-based adaptation

A common implementation involves inserting residual adapters, which are small residual bottleneck layers, between existing layers in a pretrained model. Consider a hidden representation \(\HiddenActivation\) passed between layers. A residual adapter introduces a transformation: \[ \AdaptedHiddenActivation= \HiddenActivation+ \ResidualAdapterFunction(\HiddenActivation) \] where \(\ResidualAdapterFunction(\cdot)\) is a trainable function, typically composed of two linear layers with a nonlinearity: \[ \ResidualAdapterFunction(\HiddenActivation) = \WeightMatrix_2 \, \AdapterActivationFunction(\WeightMatrix_1 \HiddenActivation) \] with \(\WeightMatrix_1 \in \mathbb{R}^{\AdapterRank\times \HiddenDimension}\) and \(\WeightMatrix_2 \in \mathbb{R}^{\HiddenDimension\times \AdapterRank}\), where \(\AdapterRank\ll \HiddenDimension\). This bottleneck design ensures that only a small number of parameters are introduced per layer.

The adapters act as learnable perturbations on top of a frozen backbone. Because they are small and sparsely applied, their weights add little storage, although their saved activations and optimizer state still count against the training budget. They let the model shift its predictions in response to new inputs.

The residual bottleneck in listing 2 maps onto the adapter equation term by term. Its down-projection plays the role of \(\WeightMatrix_1\), mapping width \(\HiddenDimension\) to the bottleneck width \(\AdapterRank\). A ReLU supplies \(\AdapterActivationFunction\), and the up-projection plays the role of \(\WeightMatrix_2\), restoring width \(\HiddenDimension\) before the input is added back. Such modules can be implemented efficiently on edge devices, particularly when the two projection matrices are small and fixed-point representations are supported.

Low-rank techniques

Another efficient strategy is to constrain the weight update itself to low rank. Low-rank adaptation (LoRA)11 leaves a base matrix \(\WeightMatrix_0 \in \mathbb{R}^{\WeightMatrixRowCount\times \WeightMatrixColumnCount}\) frozen and learns its change as the product of a down-projection \(\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\in \mathbb{R}^{\AdapterRank\times \WeightMatrixColumnCount}\) and an up-projection \(\AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\in \mathbb{R}^{\WeightMatrixRowCount\times \AdapterRank}\), with \(\AdapterRank\ll \min(\WeightMatrixRowCount,\WeightMatrixColumnCount)\). The adapted layer adds \(\AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\LayerInputVector\) to the frozen output \(\WeightMatrix_0 \LayerInputVector\), the same forward pass a serving fleet runs when many adapters share one base model. Training cost follows from that structure. The update \(\FiniteChange\WeightMatrix= \AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\) has \(\AdapterRank(\WeightMatrixRowCount+ \WeightMatrixColumnCount)\) trainable parameters instead of \(\WeightMatrixRowCount \WeightMatrixColumnCount\), and only those parameters carry gradients and optimizer state.

11 LoRA (low-rank adaptation): Hu et al. (2021) learn rank-\(\AdapterRank\) decomposition matrices \(\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\) and \(\AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\) instead of updating full weight matrices, reporting a 10,000\(\times\) reduction in trainable parameters for their GPT-3 comparison. That reduction does not apply to the full model footprint: a model with 7B parameters has about a 14 GB FP16 weight footprint before gradients, optimizer state, and activations. Even at rank 16, adapter storage depends on the dimensions and number of adapted matrices, and deployment must still accommodate the frozen base model.

Hu, Edward J., Yelong Shen, Phillip Wallis, Zeyuan Allen-Zhu, Yuanzhi Li, Shean Wang, Lu Wang, and Weizhu Chen. 2021. “LoRA: Low-Rank Adaptation of Large Language Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2106.09685.

↩︎ The low-rank adapter forward pass, in which a frozen base projection is summed with a trainable down- and up-projection, is established in Multi-LoRA serving and batched adapter execution.

The decomposition rests on a linear-algebra fact. Any matrix can be expressed as a sum of rank-one matrices through singular value decomposition. Constraining updates to low rank (typically \(\AdapterRank= 4\) to \(16\)) captures a restricted set of variation modes while reducing parameters. For a typical transformer layer with dimensions \(768{\times}768\), full fine-tuning requires updating 589,824 parameters. With rank-4 decomposition, only \(768{\times}4{\times}2 =\) 6,144 parameters are updated, a 99 percent reduction. Whether this retains most of the adaptation quality depends on the task, rank, and base model; the LoRA authors report that low-rank adapters can match or approach full fine-tuning on several transformer adaptation benchmarks (Hu et al. 2021).

LoRA was developed for transformer models but applies to any layer built on a weight matrix. From a systems engineering perspective, LoRA makes the trainable adapter the artifact that can be stored, transmitted, scheduled, and rolled back separately from the frozen base model.

Drawn to proportion for a square layer of width \(\HiddenDimension\), the adapted layer shows where the saving comes from (figure 8). Input \(\LayerInputVector\in \mathbb{R}^\HiddenDimension\) passes through the frozen \(\HiddenDimension{\times}\HiddenDimension\) base matrix \(\WeightMatrix_0\) and, in parallel, through a thin \(\AdapterRank{\times}\HiddenDimension\) down-projection \(\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\) and a \(\HiddenDimension{\times}\AdapterRank\) up-projection \(\AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\). The two paths sum to \(\HiddenActivation= \WeightMatrix_0 \LayerInputVector+ \AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\LayerInputVector\). The base weights need no parameter gradients or optimizer state, although backward computation may still traverse the base path. Folding the product into the base weights (\(\WeightMatrix_0 + \AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\)) removes separate adapter arithmetic for that merged model. Numerical compatibility, any requantization, and the cost of retaining merged copies determine whether this is the appropriate serving representation.

Consider a mobile deployment where a language model with 7B parameters has a 14 GB FP16 weight footprint, before accounting for the additional gradients, optimizer states, and activations required by full fine-tuning. That footprint exceeds smartphones with about 8 GB total memory unless the base model is quantized, partitioned, or offloaded. Choosing LoRA rank 16 restricts the update space, and this chapter’s fleet examples budget a reference adapter update of 50 MB. That figure is a communication payload, not a training-memory estimate. The device must also fit the base model, gradients, optimizer state, and live activations under its execution plan.

The smaller payload also matters under intermittent connectivity. Transferring the full FP16 model in this example requires 14 GB, while the reference adapter update is 50 MB. Transfer time depends on encoded bytes, effective link throughput, and interruptions. An adapter-only transfer provides the saving only when the receiving device already holds a compatible base model; base-version changes must be included in the communication budget. In both the residual bottleneck and LoRA, the width or rank \(\AdapterRank\) sets the trainable cost, so a deployment can size it to the memory each device class leaves for learning. The device-tier framework of section 11.4.3.4 decides which mechanism each class receives.

Edge personalization

Adapters support multiple personalized contexts with one frozen backbone. For an input width \(\HiddenDimension\), a residual bottleneck of width \(\AdapterRank\) uses \(2d\AdapterRank\) matrix parameters, plus \(\AdapterRank+\HiddenDimension\) biases when both projections include them. A deployment with several adapters must sum this count over their insertion sites; rank or bottleneck width alone does not determine the total. Stored bytes then equal parameter count times bytes per stored value. With \(\HiddenDimension= 768\) and \(\AdapterRank= 16\), one adapter holds 25,360 parameters, about 50.7 KB in FP16; one adapter after each of 12 blocks totals 304,320 parameters, roughly 0.6 MB. During training, gradients, optimizer state, and saved activations add to that storage footprint. A device can swap context-specific modules while sharing the much larger base model, provided it can hold the base and active adaptation state together.

This adapter switching pattern lets a smartphone select context-specific behavior without replacing the entire backbone. When the user enters a low-light environment, the system can load a compatible adapter into the vision pipeline; selecting an existing adapter does not itself require learning. The modularity extends naturally to federated learning: transmitting only the adapted parameters can reduce upload traffic relative to a full-model update, with savings determined by the total adapter count and encoding. Residual-adapter studies show that small trainable modules can adapt a shared visual backbone across multiple visual domains (Rebuffi et al. 2017), making them a useful design point between personalization quality and system resources. Freezing the backbone preserves its parameters, while adapters provide a bounded place for user- or context-specific updates. The resulting model can still suffer catastrophic forgetting, the loss of previously learned behavior after new updates (defined in section 11.5.2).

Rebuffi, Sylvestre-Alvise, Hakan Bilen, and Andrea Vedaldi. 2017. “Learning Multiple Visual Domains with Residual Adapters.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 30.

Napkin Math 11.2: The hidden cost of personalization
Problem: A team is deploying a 10M parameter vision model to a smartphone with support for 10 different “User Contexts” (Home, Office, Car, etc.). If a full fine-tuned model requires 40 MB, how much storage does using residual adapters save instead?

Math:

  1. Full fine-tuning: 10 contexts \(\times\) 40 MB = 400 MB.
  2. Adapter approach: (1 \(\times\) 40 MB backbone) + (10 \(\times\) 200 KB adapters) \(\approx\) 42 MB.
  3. Storage savings: 400 MB/42 MB \(\approx\) 9.5× total device storage reduction.
  4. Per-context efficiency: 40 MB/0.2 MB = 200×.

Systems insight: Multiple personalized models create a storage cost even when only one runs at a time. Storing 10 copies of a 40 MB model consumes 400 MB. In this example, sharing the frozen backbone and storing separate adapters reduces the marginal storage cost of a new context by 200×. This comparison establishes storage savings; the device must still accommodate the active model, adaptation state, and context-switching cost.

Each new context costs an adapter, not a full model copy.

In smartphone camera pipelines, environmental lighting, user preferences, and lens distortion vary between users. Residual modules can adapt a frozen shared model to these differences while leaving its base weights unchanged; the combined model’s behavior still requires validation. In voice-based systems, adapters similarly provide a way to test personalized speech recognition without retraining the full acoustic model. Keeping the base and adapter versions compatible also supports rollback or switching between user-specific versions when adaptation degrades performance.

Performance vs. resource trade-offs

Selecting an adaptation strategy requires accounting for both resource cost and expressivity. Bias-only updates retain a small trainable state but may lack the capacity to handle structural domain shifts. Full fine-tuning exposes every parameter to adaptation and adds corresponding gradient and optimizer state. LoRA and residual adapters offer intermediate choices whose cost depends on rank or width, insertion sites, and numerical representation. The same budget, equation 1, governs each of these strategies. Because peak working memory \(\AdaptationPeakMemory\) must jointly contain resident weights, trainable state \(\TrainableStateMemory\), and the peak of live activations under the device ceiling \(\DeviceMemoryCapacity\), each strategy shrinks a different term. Bias-only and adapter methods shrink \(\TrainableStateMemory\), and TinyTL-style choices also shrink the activation term. Training only the final classifier, whose backward pass stops at the head, removes most of that term. Parameter storage alone therefore cannot determine whether background adaptation fits.

LoRA and residual adapters impose different deployment costs. For a fixed adapter and compatible numerical representation, LoRA’s linear update can be merged into the backbone before inference \((\WeightMatrix_0 + \AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix)\), avoiding separate adapter arithmetic in the forward path. A quantized base may require requantization and renewed quality validation after merging. Keeping a separate merged backbone for every context also sacrifices the storage benefit of sharing one base. Residual adapters remain distinct modules (\(\LayerOutputVector= \FrozenBackboneFunction(\LayerInputVector) + \text{Adapter}(\LayerInputVector)\)); their latency cost depends on shape, placement, and runtime, while switching contexts requires only the corresponding adapter state.

The runtime must support the selected adapter’s forward operators and its training path. These can use a dynamic graph or a statically compiled update schedule; dynamic graph construction is not itself a requirement. For an inference engine such as Open Neural Network Exchange (ONNX) Runtime Mobile, a runtime that executes an adapter’s forward path may still lack its backward operators, optimizer, or state management. The deployment must verify both paths, including any transfers between the processor that runs inference and the processor that performs updates.

For this multi-context personalization workload, where one device switches among several adapters, system architects can use a device-tier framework to organize candidate mechanisms. On flagship phones (Tier 1) with dedicated Neural Engines and 8 GB of RAM or more, residual adapters provide a strong balance of modularity and speed, because switching contexts swaps only adapter state. On mid-range devices (Tier 2) with 1 GB up to that ceiling, LoRA may fit when a fixed adapter can be merged into a compatible base representation before inference, trading switching flexibility for zero added inference arithmetic. For ultra-constrained IoT endpoints (Tier 3) with less than 1 GB RAM, bias-only updates may be the only practical option. Budget phones show that the tier follows the memory available to learning rather than installed RAM. Their 4 GB places them in Tier 2, yet only 1 GB–2 GB remains for ML work after the operating system. A background adaptation task may be granted less, so a deployment can reasonably hold them to the Tier 3 mechanism. The assignments follow from the workload as much as from the hardware. A workload that keeps one adaptation per user, rather than many switchable contexts, loses the reason to keep adapters separate and can favor merged LoRA even on flagship devices. The runtime must still verify the base-model footprint and admit training within the measured device envelope.

Checkpoint 11.2: Edge personalization

Verify understanding of efficient on-device adaptation:

11.4.4 Sparse updates

Bias-only updates and fixed adapters choose where the model may change before seeing local data, yet a local shift can alter specific convolutional filters or attention channels rather than every block uniformly. Sparse updates instead adapt selected existing parameters rather than adding modules. Their expressivity relative to adapters depends on the selected locations and parameter budget. A task-adaptive policy selects those locations using evidence about the current task or user. The resulting update space can target changes that a fixed subset misses, but selection itself consumes resources and does not guarantee that the complete training path fits the device.

The useful savings depend on what the runtime can omit. Unselected parameters need no gradient or optimizer state, but their operations may still lie on the backward path to selected parameters. A policy must therefore compare the measured update cost with its adaptation benefit; the fraction of trainable parameters alone does not predict the reduction in compute. Because layers do not contribute equally to local performance gains, identifying the minimal subset with the highest adaptation value lets the system spend scarce training capacity where it changes the model’s behavior most.

Sparse update design

Let a neural network be defined by parameters \(\ModelParameters= \{\ModelParameters_1, \ModelParameters_2, \ldots, \ModelParameters_{\LayerCount}\}\) across \(\LayerCount\) layers. In standard fine-tuning, gradients are computed and updates are performed on all parameters: \[ \ModelParameters_i \leftarrow \ModelParameters_i - \LearningRate\frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \ModelParameters_i}, \quad \text{for } \LayerSelectionIndex= 1, \ldots, \LayerCount \]

In task-adaptive sparse updates, the system selects a small subset \(\SelectedLayerSet\subset \{1, \ldots, \LayerCount\}\) such that only parameters in \(\SelectedLayerSet\) are updated: \[ \ModelParameters_i \leftarrow \begin{cases} \ModelParameters_i - \LearningRate\frac{\partial \LossFunction}{\partial \ModelParameters_i}, & \text{if } \LayerSelectionIndex\in \SelectedLayerSet\\ \ModelParameters_i, & \text{otherwise} \end{cases} \]

The challenge lies in selecting the optimal subset \(\SelectedLayerSet\) given memory and compute constraints. A principled strategy is contribution analysis, an empirical method that estimates how much each layer contributes to downstream performance improvement. For example, one can measure the marginal gain from updating each layer independently:

  1. Freeze the entire model.
  2. Unfreeze one candidate layer.
  3. Finetune briefly and evaluate improvement in validation accuracy.
  4. Rank layers by performance gain per unit cost (e.g., per KB of trainable memory).

This layer-wise profiling yields a candidate ranking from which \(\SelectedLayerSet\) can be constructed subject to a memory budget. Gains measured one layer at a time need not add when layers train together, and shared backward dependencies change the combined cost. The selected set therefore needs a joint quality and resource check, with the profiling cost included in the adaptation budget.

In a complete selective fine-tuning pipeline, that ranking is the middle of three stages (figure 9). Stage 1 meta-trains the network in the cloud, which means training its initial weights so that a few later updates on new data adapt it well (the idea behind model-agnostic meta-learning, taken up in section 11.7.3). Stage 2 ranks layers or channel groups by adaptation benefit per unit of device cost and lets the device budget set the cut, and Stage 3 updates only the units above the cut on local data. Freezing the units below the cut removes their gradient and optimizer state, but the backward pass still runs through any frozen layer that lies between the loss and a selected one. The selected set must therefore be costed under the complete execution schedule.

A concrete example is TinyTrain, a method designed to allow rapid adaptation on-device (Kwon et al. 2024). TinyTrain combines offline preparation with a task-adaptive sparse-update method that dynamically selects layers or channels to update using user data, memory limits, and compute limits. At runtime, the system updates only the selected subset rather than treating the whole network as trainable.

In implementation, selective layer updating turns the ranking into requires_grad decisions or equivalent graph masks. In the profiling-driven version, contribution scores and the memory limit decide which layers are unfrozen (listing 3).

Consider a user wearing an augmented reality headset that performs real-time object recognition. As lighting and environments shift, the system must adapt to maintain accuracy, but training must occur during brief idle periods or while charging. TinyTrain addresses this constraint with task-adaptive sparse updates, in which the deployment process selects a small update set that fits the device’s memory and compute budget. The approach keeps adaptation fast, energy-efficient, and memory-aware when the target task and device profile match the method’s assumptions.

Task-adaptive sparse updates still introduce system-level trade-offs. Contribution analysis has a nontrivial computational cost even when it occurs during pretraining or initial profiling, so the deployment pipeline must budget for it. Stability also requires attention: if too few parameters are selected for updating, the model may underfit the target distribution, so teams need validation thresholds for the selected subset before deployment. The selection policy must also account for hardware-specific execution costs because some parameters show high contribution scores but prove expensive to update on certain architectures. Despite these trade-offs, task-adaptive sparse updates provide a practical mechanism to scale adaptation across deployment contexts from microcontrollers to mobile devices (Kwon et al. 2024).

Kwon, Y. D., R. Li, S. I. Venieris, J. Chauhan, and N. D. Lane. 2024. “TinyTrain: Resource-Aware Task-Adaptive Sparse Training of DNNs at the Data-Scarce Edge.” Proceedings of the 41st International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML).

Adaptation strategy comparison

The design target is the smallest update space that captures the local change. Full fine-tuning pays every term of equation 1. Bias-only updates and head-only training fit minor calibration or new output categories. LoRA and residual adapters add bounded capacity beside a frozen backbone. Task-adaptive sparse updates spend profiling effort to select valuable existing parameters. A larger update space is justified only if the measured quality gain fits the device’s training budget. Each strategy shrinks a different budget term, and the choice must still be checked against expressivity, runtime support, and selection cost. Table 4 sets these adaptation strategy trade-offs side by side.

Once an adaptation mechanism fits the device’s memory and execution budget, it must still learn from the available examples. Reducing trainable state does not create labels or ensure that a user’s recent observations represent future use. The next constraint is therefore statistical: extracting reliable learning signal from sparse local data while retaining useful prior behavior.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why does Tiny Transfer Learning (TinyTL) freeze convolutional backbone weights and adapt biases with optional lite residual modules?

    1. Freezing removes backbone weight-gradient and optimizer state; suitable activation and normalization choices also reduce saved backward state.
    2. Microcontroller hardware units physically short-circuit if matrix multiplications are performed in reverse during backpropagation.
    3. Bias parameters have mathematical properties that prevent all forms of numerical underflow in 8-bit fixed-point arithmetic.
    4. Training bias parameters modifies the underlying kernel weights directly through implicit singular value decomposition.

  2. A mobile vision engineer chooses between Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) and Residual Bottleneck Adapters for on-device camera personalization. The system requires zero additional inference latency during real-time 60 FPS video capture, while another use case requires hot-swapping between 10 user contexts without reloading the base model. How do LoRA and Residual Adapters compare across these requirements?

    1. Residual Adapters introduce zero inference latency because they replace the backbone, while LoRA requires recomputing matrix inverses on every video frame.
    2. LoRA updates can be merged into compatible base weights to remove the adapter execution path, while residual adapters retain separate computation and support modular context switching.
    3. LoRA updates require storing 10 full copies of the 7B base model in SRAM, whereas Residual Adapters operate without trainable parameters.
    4. Both methods require identical memory footprints and add exactly 50 ms of inference latency per layer.

  3. A smart camera supports 10 personalization contexts. Its base model has 10M parameters (\(40\text{ MB}\) in FP32), and assume each complete adapter checkpoint occupies \(200\text{ KB}\). Compare storage for 10 full fine-tuned models with one shared base plus 10 adapters. Explain the architectural advantage.

  4. In structured parameter adaptation, a technique that approximates a full weight matrix update \(\FiniteChange\WeightMatrix \in \mathbb{R}^{\WeightMatrixRowCount \times \WeightMatrixColumnCount}\) as the product of a low-rank up-projection \(\AdapterUpProjectionMatrix \in \mathbb{R}^{\WeightMatrixRowCount \times \AdapterRank}\) and down-projection \(\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix \in \mathbb{R}^{\AdapterRank \times \WeightMatrixColumnCount}\) (where \(\AdapterRank \ll \min(\WeightMatrixRowCount, \WeightMatrixColumnCount)\)) is known as ____ (low-rank adaptation).

  5. Task-adaptive sparse updates use individual-layer profiling to propose a trainable subset. Order the steps from profiling to deployment:

  1. Fine-tune each candidate layer separately and measure its validation gain and resource cost
  2. Freeze the pretrained model to establish a baseline
  3. Deploy the jointly validated subset with other parameters frozen
  4. Rank layers by gain per unit cost and propose a subset
  5. Measure the selected combination against memory, energy, foreground-latency, and quality budgets, including profiling cost

11.5 Data Efficiency

A user corrects smartphone keyboard autocorrect perhaps three times a day. The system cannot require 10,000 labeled sentences to train a local language model. Data efficiency at the edge means learning aggressively from a sparse, noisy, and uncurated trickle of implicit user feedback, maximizing the signal extracted from each interaction.

The systems trade-off is data collection cost against adaptation quality. Every retained example costs user friction, energy, storage, and privacy exposure. A voice assistant adapting to speech samples weighs model improvement against battery drain and user comfort with always-on recording, and a keyboard chooses between logging shallow keystroke statistics and keeping full typing sessions. Unlike a central pipeline, the device cannot reach into a larger dataset when its own evidence runs short, and an application that needs a decision before enough evidence accumulates may be better served by a validated fixed model than by immediate learning. Whatever the device does learn must also stay compatible with the adaptation mechanism of section 11.4 and with any federated protocol that later consumes it.

The strategy choice depends on which scarcity binds first, and practical systems usually combine several: few-shot learning handles cases where only a handful of labeled or weakly labeled examples must drive personalization, whereas streaming updates handle observations that arrive incrementally and cannot be batched in advance. Because continual streaming updates can overwrite earlier behavior, experience replay spends memory to retain and reuse scarce examples, while data compression shrinks those stored histories so that replay and lightweight training remain feasible within edge memory budgets. Data scarcity thus becomes an allocation problem, in which the device spends labels, memory, compute, and privacy budget where they produce the most reliable local signal.

11.5.1 Few-shot learning and data streaming

A user enrolling a custom wake word will record five audio samples during setup, not five thousand, and subsequent corrections arrive one interaction at a time during ordinary use. Because local learning must proceed from either a tiny enrollment set or a passive trickle of user interactions and ambient sensor readings, it relies on two complementary adaptation strategies: few-shot learning, in which models generalize from a small, static set of examples, and streaming adaptation, where incremental observations support updates scheduled within device budgets.

Few-shot adaptation uses limited labeled or weakly labeled evidence for a new task or user condition (Wang et al. 2020). Bias-only updates, adapter modules, and prototype-based classification restrict what must be learned, but each still needs validation against overfitting. Let \(\FewShotSupportSet= \{(\ModelInput_i, \ExampleLabel_i)\}_{i=1}^{\SupportSetSize}\) denote the local support set; here \(\SupportSetSize\) counts all examples in the set, rather than examples per class. A candidate policy might permit few gradient steps (\(\FewShotStepCount\ll 100\)) and a small trainable subset (\(|\UpdatedParameterSubset| \ll |\ModelParameters|\)). These are illustrative restrictions, not universal feasibility conditions. The actual step and parameter budgets follow from measured device cost and the quality target, including retained performance on earlier tasks.

Wang, Y., Q. Yao, J. T. Kwok, and L. M. Ni. 2020. “Generalizing from a Few Examples: A Survey on Few-Shot Learning.” ACM Computing Surveys 53 (3): 1–34. https://doi.org/10.1145/3386252.
Warden, Pete. 2018. “Speech Commands: A Dataset for Limited-Vocabulary Speech Recognition.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1804.03209, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.1804.03209.

Keyword spotting (KWS) systems are a common target for data-efficient on-device adaptation; Speech Commands provides a dataset for training and evaluating limited-vocabulary KWS models (Warden 2018). A detector must distinguish its target phrase from background speech while meeting latency and false-activation requirements. One design uses a pretrained acoustic encoder, such as a small convolutional or recurrent network, to map audio into embeddings for a lightweight classifier. A shared encoder supplies reusable features, while local examples can support a custom wake word or an underrepresented accent. Whether those examples justify local training depends on their coverage, transfer restrictions, and the improvement over the shared detector.

Few-shot adaptation can train an output classifier or another small parameter subset from utterances collected directly on the device. A user might provide 5–10 recordings of a custom wake word while the acoustic encoder remains frozen. Those positive examples define what to recognize, but the false-activation rate on other speech and background noise is a separate measurement, and the personalized detector should not be accepted until it has been made.

Head-only adaptation is cheap in a way the peak-memory budget of equation 1 makes precise. Because the backward pass stops at the classifier, the encoder’s saved activations leave the activation term, and the encoder outputs for the support set can be computed once and reused across update steps. What remains is running the encoder forward, holding the support examples or their features, and evaluating the adapted classifier. Those costs, not the size of the head, decide whether the update fits a particular phone or microcontroller.

Many on-device scenarios instead call for streaming adaptation, in which the model learns incrementally as data arrives over time rather than from a fixed support set (Hayes et al. 2020). Let \(\{\ModelInput_t\}_{t=1}^{\infty}\) represent the observations selected from the stream for admitted updates. In streaming settings, observations arrive continuously, but the runtime admits training only when memory, energy, and foreground-latency budgets permit. The following rule describes one admitted update; it does not require a gradient step on every arrival. The index \(\OptimizerStepIndex\) counts these update steps. The computable loss may come from implicit feedback, pseudo-labels, self-supervised objectives, reconstruction error, or a task-specific proxy rather than explicit human labels. The model update can be written generically as: \[ \ModelParameters_{t+1} = \ModelParameters_t - \LearningRate_t \nabla \LossFunction(\ModelInput_t; \ModelParameters_t) \] where \(\LearningRate_t\) is the learning rate at time \(\OptimizerStepIndex\). This form of adaptation is sensitive to noise and drift in the input distribution, and thus often incorporates mechanisms such as learning rate decay, meta-learned initialization, or update gating to improve stability.

The same strategies apply beyond keyword spotting. In wearable health devices, a model that classifies physical activities may begin with a generic classifier and adapt to user-specific motion patterns using only a few labeled activity segments. In smart assistants, user voice profiles are fine-tuned over time using ongoing speech input, even when explicit supervision is unavailable. Local corrections, repetitions, or downstream task completion can provide implicit signals, but only after the system defines which event counts as feedback and tests its validity. A user may accept a suggestion because it is convenient rather than correct, abandon a task for reasons unrelated to the model, or correct an error that the model itself induced. Production adaptation should therefore separate observed events from inferred labels, retain a nonadapting control cohort, measure delayed outcomes when available, and gate learning when the proxy-to-outcome relationship drifts. A stream of such updates also raises a stability problem that few-shot and streaming rules do not address on their own.

11.5.2 Experience replay

When a streaming update rule trains only on a user’s newest interactions, gradient steps on recent examples overwrite the weights that recognized earlier classes, causing accuracy on prior tasks to collapse within a few updates. Retaining a small sample of earlier observations on the device directly counters this failure mode.

Definition 11.3: Catastrophic forgetting

Catastrophic forgetting is the phenomenon in which a neural network trained sequentially on new tasks or data distributions rapidly loses performance on previously learned tasks, because gradient updates that optimize the new objective overwrite weight configurations that supported the old ones.

  1. Significance: Without explicit mitigation, fine-tuning a deployed classifier on a new domain can sharply reduce prior-task accuracy, even when the new domain is closely related to the old one (Kirkpatrick et al. 2017). This is the central failure mode of on-device and federated learning. Every device that adapts locally to its user’s recent data risks erasing the general capabilities the centralized training run paid for, and the loss is invisible until inference encounters out-of-recent-context inputs.
  2. Distinction: Unlike overfitting (a generalization failure on a single fixed task) and distribution shift (a deployment-time mismatch between training and inference data), forgetting is a training-time phenomenon caused by the sequential structure of the update stream itself. Updates for new data can interfere with earlier solutions even when a representation capable of supporting both tasks exists.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that a small experience-replay buffer is sufficient protection. A small buffer can hold aggregate accuracy on a held-out set while rare classes, which it samples too seldom, continue to degrade. Forgetting must therefore be measured per class or per cohort, and the remedies range from capacity-proportional or prioritized buffers to weight-regularization methods such as elastic weight consolidation, which store no examples (section 11.8.2).

Learning a new task can erode accuracy on an earlier one.

Mnih, Volodymyr, Koray Kavukcuoglu, David Silver, Andrei A. Rusu, Joel Veness, Marc G. Bellemare, Alex Graves, et al. 2015. “Human-Level Control Through Deep Reinforcement Learning.” Nature 518 (7540): 529–33. https://doi.org/10.1038/nature14236.

Experience replay addresses forgetting by retaining examples from earlier learning episodes and reusing them during later updates. Replay also appears in reinforcement learning (Mnih et al. 2015); here its role is to keep earlier local observations represented as new data arrives. It is one stability mechanism with an explicit storage cost. Section 11.8.2 returns to the problem with parameter-importance penalties, bounded update footprints, and rollback.

Unlike server-side replay strategies that rely on large datasets and extensive compute, on-device replay must operate with extremely limited capacity, often with tens or hundreds of samples, and must avoid interfering with user experience (Rolnick et al. 2019). Buffers may store only compressed features or distilled summaries, and updates must occur opportunistically (e.g., during idle cycles or charging). These system-level constraints reshape how replay is implemented and evaluated in the context of embedded ML.

Rolnick, David, Arun Ahuja, Jonathan Schwarz, Timothy Lillicrap, and Greg Wayne. 2019. “Experience Replay for Continual Learning.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS).

Let \(\ReplayBuffer\) represent a memory buffer that retains a fixed-size subset of training examples. At time step \(\OptimizerStepIndex\), the model receives a new data point \((\ModelInput_t, \ExampleLabel_t)\) and appends it to \(\ReplayBuffer\). A replay-based update then samples a batch \(\{(\ModelInput_i, \ExampleLabel_i)\}_{i=1}^{\BatchSize}\) of size \(\BatchSize\) from \(\ReplayBuffer\) and applies a gradient step: \[ \ModelParameters_{t+1} = \ModelParameters_t - \LearningRate\nabla_\ModelParameters\left[ \frac{1}{\BatchSize} \sum_{i=1}^{\BatchSize} \LossFunction(\ModelInput_i, \ExampleLabel_i; \ModelParameters_t) \right] \] This is the streaming update of section 11.5.1 with one change of data source, from the newest observation to a batch drawn from \(\ReplayBuffer\). The systems cost lies in what that change requires, namely buffer storage that competes with model state and forward and backward work on old examples in every admitted window. Over time, this replay mechanism allows the model to reinforce prior knowledge while incorporating new information.

A practical on-device implementation might use a ring buffer to store a small set of compressed feature vectors rather than full input examples. The buffer has a fixed capacity and overwrites its oldest entry once full, so one parameter bounds its memory and sets the balance between retained history and new data (listing 4).

This implementation maintains a fixed-capacity cyclic buffer, storing compressed representations (e.g., last-layer embeddings) and associated labels. Uniform sampling here covers only the retained recent window; it does not remove the history bias introduced by overwriting old examples. Reservoir sampling is an alternative when the goal is to represent the full stream, though any finite buffer still requires coverage checks.

In TinyML applications, experience replay has been applied to problems such as gesture recognition, where devices must continuously improve predictions while observing a small number of events per day. Instead of training directly on the streaming data, the device stores representative feature vectors from recent gestures and uses them to finetune classification boundaries periodically. Similarly, in on-device keyword spotting, replaying past utterances can improve wake-word detection accuracy without the need to transmit audio data off-device.

Experience replay spends storage and additional update work to preserve earlier behavior. Raw inputs can exceed storage or retention budgets, especially in vision and audio applications. Cached feature vectors reduce that footprint, but an update starting from those vectors trains the downstream model without reconstructing the original encoder computation. If the encoder changes, old features may no longer match its current representation; the system must retain a compatible encoder, refresh the features from retained inputs, or expire incompatible entries. Persistent replay also consumes flash write cycles. Buffer contents, representation versions, and replay frequency therefore belong in the same resource and retention policy.

11.5.3 Data compression

Experience replay is limited by how many examples fit in the buffer: storing raw \(224{\times}224\) camera frames or uncompressed audio waveforms inside an on-device replay buffer fills a wearable’s or microcontroller’s memory after only a few dozen observations. When raw training inputs are too large, noisy, or redundant to store and process directly, transforming them into lower-dimensional embeddings or compact encodings preserves task-relevant structure while shrinking memory and compute costs. Compressed representations serve two complementary goals. They reduce the footprint of stored data, allowing devices to maintain longer histories or replay buffers under tight memory budgets (Hayes et al. 2020). They also simplify the learning task by projecting raw inputs into more structured feature spaces, often learned via pretraining or meta-learning, in which efficient adaptation is possible with minimal supervision.

Hayes, T. L., K. Kafle, R. Shrestha, M. Acharya, and C. Kanan. 2020. “REMIND Your Neural Network to Prevent Catastrophic Forgetting.” In Computer Vision – ECCV 2020. Springer. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-58598-3_28.

One common approach is to encode data points using a pretrained feature extractor and discard the original high-dimensional input. For example, an image \(\ModelInput_i\) might be passed through a CNN to produce an embedding vector \(\StoredEmbedding_i = \FrozenFeatureEncoder(\ModelInput_i)\), where \(\FrozenFeatureEncoder(\cdot)\) is a fixed feature encoder. This embedding captures visual structure (e.g., shape, texture, or spatial layout) in a compact representation, usually ranging from 64 to 512 dimensions, suitable for lightweight downstream adaptation.

Mathematically, training can proceed over compressed samples \((\StoredEmbedding_i, \ExampleLabel_i)\) using a lightweight decoder or projection head. Let \(\ModelParameters\) represent the trainable parameters of this decoder model, which is typically a small neural network that maps from compressed representations to output predictions. As each example is presented, the model parameters are updated using gradient descent: \[ \ModelParameters_{t+1} = \ModelParameters_t - \LearningRate\nabla_\ModelParameters\LossFunction\big(\PredictionHeadFunction(\StoredEmbedding_i; \ModelParameters), \ExampleLabel_i\big) \] The rule is the same gradient step once more, now applied to stored embeddings \(\StoredEmbedding_i\) and a small head whose prediction is \(\PredictionHeadFunction(\StoredEmbedding_i; \ModelParameters)\), rather than to raw inputs. Only the head’s parameters \(\ModelParameters\) change, so this is the head-only case of equation 1, and the encoder runs once per example when the embedding is stored rather than on every update. This bounds the trainable portion of the model, but feasibility still depends on the head’s activations and optimizer state alongside the replay buffer and runtime reserves.

When replay storage rather than model compute binds, a device can go further and learn a dictionary of recurring sensor-trace patterns, storing each example as a few nonzero coefficients over that dictionary instead of as raw samples. A dataset of traces \(\SensorTraceMatrix\) then factorizes as \(\SensorTraceMatrix\approx \SensorPatternDictionary\DictionaryCoefficientMatrix\), where the columns of \(\SensorPatternDictionary\) are the basis patterns and the block-sparse coefficient matrix \(\DictionaryCoefficientMatrix\) records which patterns are active in each example. The saving is real only if the dictionary plus all stored coefficients is smaller than the raw replay examples. Updating the dictionary changes the representation of every stored example, so reconstruction quality and compatibility become part of the replay policy.

Compressed representations can reduce how long raw inputs remain stored, but an embedding is not anonymized merely because it is lower dimensional. It may preserve identity, attributes, or enough structure for inversion, and it must receive the same access-control, retention, and deletion analysis as the source data unless a separate privacy mechanism establishes a stronger guarantee. Compression can also act as an implicit regularizer, smoothing learning and mitigating overfitting when only a few training examples are available.

In keyword spotting, raw audio can first be transformed into mel-frequency cepstral coefficients (MFCCs),12 a compact, lossy representation derived from the speech power spectrum. Storing these vectors instead of waveforms reduces the replay payload. The complete adaptation budget must still include feature extraction, model state, and the working memory needed for updates.

12 MFCCs (mel-frequency cepstral coefficients): For a 320-sample audio window (20 ms at 16 kHz), 16-bit samples occupy 640 bytes, while 12–13 coefficients stored at 32 bits each occupy approximately 50 bytes. The roughly 13\(\times\) reduction compares one waveform window with one feature vector; overlapping windows, additional features, and metadata change the storage ratio for a complete recording. Replay capacity depends on that complete representation, and keyword detection quality must be evaluated separately from byte savings.

11.5.4 Data efficiency strategy comparison

Each of these three mechanisms spends a different scarce resource: replaying raw observations when memory is the bottleneck overflows device storage, whereas compressing inputs into fixed embeddings when the feature extractor itself needs to adapt freezes the wrong representation. Few-shot adaptation uses a small informative support set, replay spends memory and compute to preserve prior behavior, and compressed representations extend the history that fits on-device. Their limits remain different: weak pretrained features can defeat few-shot adaptation, a replay buffer can omit rare cases, and compression can discard task-relevant information. Combining mechanisms does not escape these on-device learning trade-offs (table 5), because every combination must fit the same memory, energy, and privacy budget.

In practice, these methods are not mutually exclusive. Many real-world systems combine them to achieve robust, efficient adaptation. For example, a keyword spotting system may use compressed audio features (e.g., MFCCs), finetune a few parameters from a small support set, and maintain a replay buffer of past embeddings for continual refinement.

Efficient adaptation and data strategies establish how a single device can learn from its user. Sharing those gains requires another decision: whether local updates contain information useful to a common model and can be combined within communication and privacy constraints. Federated learning supplies that coordination protocol. Personal state can remain local when sharing would not improve the population objective, and privacy controls must separately limit what transmitted updates disclose.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A developer of a keyword spotting (KWS) system for smart home appliances wants users to configure custom wake words using 5 to 10 spoken samples. Why is few-shot adaptation of a classification head on top of a frozen acoustic encoder preferred over end-to-end retraining?

    1. End-to-end retraining of acoustic encoders produces audio waveforms that violate device speaker impedance limits.
    2. Spoken custom wake words cannot be processed by neural networks unless the entire encoder architecture is expanded by \(10\times\).
    3. Updating only the head restricts trainable state and preserves frozen encoder representations, reducing adaptation cost and overfitting risk relative to full retraining.
    4. The Fourier transform algorithm only converges when supervised labels exceed 100,000 samples.

  2. An edge health tracker adapts a continuous activity classifier to a user’s walking gait over several weeks. When the user starts cycling, the device adapts its model, but suddenly misclassifies normal walking as cycling. What core failure mode has occurred, and why did it happen?

    1. Overfitting on a single static batch caused by excessive L2 regularization.
    2. Numerical floating-point overflow in the hardware accelerometer sensor buffer.
    3. Model inversion attack where cycling data erased the operating system kernel.
    4. Catastrophic forgetting, where sequential gradient updates on new data distributions overwrite the weight configurations responsible for preserving performance on previously learned tasks.

  3. In on-device continuous learning, why do production experience-replay implementations store compressed feature embeddings (e.g., penultimate-layer activations or MFCCs) in a circular ring buffer rather than saving raw sensor observations (such as raw images or audio waveforms)?

  4. True or False: In on-device experience replay, maintaining a small buffer of 100 uniformly sampled past examples guarantees that the adapted model will not suffer from catastrophic forgetting across rare or long-tail edge cases.

  5. In continuous edge adaptation, the fundamental trade-off between retaining previously acquired knowledge and acquiring new patterns from dynamic local data is known as the stability-____ trade-off.

See Answers →

11.6 Federated Learning Algorithms

The voice assistant of the chapter’s opening, now deployed to millions of homes whose intermittently available devices contribute updates when local conditions permit, shows what local adaptation alone cannot do. One household pronounces “data” as “DAY-tuh” and another as “DA-tuh”; a third, a technical household, says “machine learning” every day, while a fourth never has. Local adaptation can specialize each model to its own household, but nothing it learns travels. If the fourth household starts using the phrase, its model must learn it from scratch, even though millions of other devices already handle it. Federation offers that transfer when the observations support a common objective. The system must decide which changes improve shared representations and which should remain personal, then evaluate whether aggregation helps the intended users.

Federated learning (definition 11.2) supplies that transfer while each device keeps its raw examples local. The approach turns data locality into one privacy control, not a complete privacy guarantee. A system must still treat gradients, model deltas, participation timing, device identifiers, and aggregate statistics as sensitive outputs because these signals can reveal information about local users (Kairouz and McMahan 2021; Zhu et al. 2019). Of the three architectures in figure 2, only federated learning pools experience across users without moving raw examples, and it buys that position with a protocol.

11.6.1 Cross-device and cross-silo federations

Before choosing a federated optimization or privacy protocol, an architect must specify who the participants are, because a mobile cohort-sampling protocol applied to ten collaborating hospitals breaks scheduling, weighting, and fault recovery, as does a hospital consortium’s stable round membership expected from millions of intermittently connected phones. The voice assistant is cross-device, with millions of unreliable phones, whereas a group of hospitals that want to train a complication-prediction model without centralizing patient records is cross-silo, with a few reliable institutions that each hold a large dataset. That distinction changes the failure model, scheduling policy, and privacy unit before any optimizer is selected (table 6) (Kairouz and McMahan 2021).

11.6.2 Learning protocols

Once the participant population is defined, a federated round must coordinate devices that hold different data distributions, connect over constrained links, and drop out unpredictably. If devices trained on unaligned starting versions and uploaded raw weight tensors whenever they came online, the coordinator would sum incompatible parameter spaces and stall on offline stragglers. Practical distributed coordination protocols must therefore solve three engineering challenges simultaneously. Local training must produce compatible updates despite non-IID data distributions. Aggregation must combine those updates efficiently despite bandwidth constraints. Timing must be coordinated across devices with heterogeneous availability patterns.

Local training

Local training turns the device’s adaptation policy into a contribution to a shared model. Every contribution needs a known starting version, a compatible parameter schema, and a declared local update rule. Otherwise, arrays with the same shape may describe changes to different models. A synchronous protocol therefore starts every participating client from the same shared version, trains on local examples under that rule, and returns the difference from the starting parameters, while personal parameters stay outside the aggregate.

The process repeats with a new shared version for each accepted round. Update frequency depends on device availability, local work, and communication cost. A secure transmission channel protects the transfer, but the receiving server can still inspect the update unless the aggregation protocol separately hides individual contributions. Model compatibility and update confidentiality are therefore distinct protocol obligations.

Federated aggregation protocols

The most widely used rule for combining client updates is federated averaging (FedAvg), which has become a canonical algorithm for federated learning (McMahan et al. 2017). In FedAvg, each device trains its local copy of the model using SGD on its private data. The algorithm is local SGD, which synchronizes only after several local steps to save communication, run with two changes that the data center version never faces. Only a sampled subset of clients participates in each round, and each client’s data is a non-IID shard of the population. The local steps that save communication therefore also pull each client toward its own optimum.

↩︎ Local SGD and the trade-off between synchronization frequency and convergence that it exposes are established in Scaling Efficiency and Convergence.

Formally, let \(\ClientLocalDataset_k\) denote the local dataset on client \(\ClientIndex\), and let \(\ModelParameters_k^\FederatedRoundIndex\) be the parameters of the model on client \(\ClientIndex\) at round \(\FederatedRoundIndex\). Each client performs \(\LocalEpochCount\) epochs of SGD on its local data, yielding an update \(\ModelParameters_k^{\FederatedRoundIndex+1}\). The central server then aggregates these updates as: \[ \ModelParameters^{\FederatedRoundIndex+1} = \sum_{k=1}^{\ParticipatingClientCount} \frac{\ClientSampleCount_k}{\RoundSampleCount} \ModelParameters_k^{\FederatedRoundIndex+1} \tag{3}\] where \(\ClientSampleCount_k = |\ClientLocalDataset_k|\) is the number of samples on device \(\ClientIndex\), \(\RoundSampleCount= \sum_k \ClientSampleCount_k\) is the total number of samples across participating clients, and \(\ParticipatingClientCount\) is the number of active devices in the current round. Here \(\ParticipatingClientCount\) is a client count, unrelated to the C³ axes, and bare \(\LocalEpochCount\) counts local epochs; the energy quantities of equation 2 always carry a subscript.

This formula optimizes an example-weighted objective, in which a client with ten times as many local examples receives ten times the influence. That choice is appropriate when every example should contribute equally, but it can let prolific users or large institutions dominate the model. A user-weighted objective instead assigns each participating client weight \(1/\ParticipatingClientCount\), optionally capping or normalizing each client’s local contribution before aggregation. Neither choice is universally fair. The system must declare whether its unit of interest is an example, a user, an organization, or a deployment cohort, then evaluate that population rather than inherit \(\ClientSampleCount_k/\RoundSampleCount\) as an implementation default.

A deployed round wraps the averaging rule in policy that the equation leaves unstated (figure 10). Equation 3 fixes how completed models combine, but not which clients are invited, how long the coordinator waits, or how few completions force a round to be abandoned. Those protocol choices decide which contributions enter the sum. A client that misses the deadline contributes nothing, and its examples are absent from \(\RoundSampleCount\) for that round. Over-selection and its population-coverage trade-off are developed in section 11.7.1.

This basic structure introduces a number of design choices and trade-offs. The number of local epochs \(\LocalEpochCount\) sets the balance between computation and communication. A larger \(\LocalEpochCount\) reduces communication frequency but risks divergence if local data distributions vary too much. The selection of participating clients affects convergence stability and fairness. In real-world deployments, not all devices are available at all times, and hardware capabilities may differ substantially, requiring robust participation scheduling and failure tolerance.

⇄ Contrast: Federated rounds over intermittent wide-area clients contrast with the synchronous AllReduce collectives on low-latency cluster fabrics established in AllReduce Algorithms and Topologies.

Time to target under client drift

A federated deployment must reach a quality target within its time, energy, and participation budgets. More local training can reduce how often clients communicate, but it also changes their updates and the time each selected cohort takes to finish. The engineering comparison is therefore time to an agreed quality target, not communication rounds alone.

What the FedAvg controls count

FedAvg exposes the number of participating clients \(\ParticipatingClientCount\), local epochs \(\LocalEpochCount\), and communication rounds \(\FederatedRoundCount\) as controls (McMahan et al. 2017). Their product \(\ParticipatingClientCount \LocalEpochCount \FederatedRoundCount\) counts client-epochs when participation is fixed, which makes it a tally of the work a run performs rather than a measure of its progress. Clients can have different dataset sizes, and equal client-epoch counts can process different examples or produce conflicting updates, so two runs with the same \(\ParticipatingClientCount \LocalEpochCount \FederatedRoundCount\) can end at very different quality. A convergence result is useful only when its assumptions match the optimizer, client sampling, and data distributions.

McMahan, Brendan, Eider Moore, Daniel Ramage, Seth Hampson, and Blaise Agüera y Arcas. 2017. “Communication-Efficient Learning of Deep Networks from Decentralized Data.” International Conference on Artificial Intelligence and Statistics (AISTATS), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 54: 1273–82.
Non-IID data impact

Non-IID data is where federated averaging departs from the data center version of local SGD. Each local step moves a client toward the optimum of its own shard. After many local steps the client models sit near different optima, and their weighted average lands at a point that need not minimize the population objective. This client drift grows with the number of local steps and with how far the client distributions differ, and it degrades federated averaging relative to i.i.d. baselines (Li et al. 2020; Zhao et al. 2018). It therefore eats into the benefit of fewer communications exactly where communication is most expensive. How strongly depends on the partition, update rule, participation policy, and local step size. No universal heterogeneity score predicts a round count across applications, which is why section 11.6.2.3.3 measures the trade-off rather than computing it.

When statistical heterogeneity causes FedAvg to diverge or stall, two foundational algorithmic extensions address the root cause of client drift:

  1. FedProx (Li et al. 2020) adds a proximal penalty proportional to \(\|\ModelParameters - \GlobalModelParameters^{\FederatedRoundIndex}\|^2\) to each client’s local loss function. This penalty constrains local updates to remain in the neighborhood of the current global model \(\GlobalModelParameters^{\FederatedRoundIndex}\), stabilizing convergence under statistical heterogeneity and permitting devices to execute variable numbers of local epochs without destabilizing the server aggregate.
  2. SCAFFOLD (Kairouz and McMahan 2021) corrects the local update direction directly using control variates. By maintaining a server control variate and per-client control variates that estimate the difference between client-local and global gradient directions, SCAFFOLD applies an additive correction to each local SGD step. This correction counters client drift under heterogeneous local data, although it roughly doubles the communication volume per round by transmitting control variates alongside model parameters.
Measuring the local-work trade-off

Compare candidate local-epoch settings under the same initialization, data partition, participation policy, and held-out quality target. For each setting \(\LocalEpochCount\), record the first validated round \(\FederatedRoundCount(\LocalEpochCount)\) that reaches the target and the elapsed duration \(\RoundDuration(\LocalEpochCount)\) of each completed round, including client selection, local work, transfer, aggregation, and any waiting. The observed time to target is

\[ \TimeToTarget(\LocalEpochCount) = \sum_{\CompletedRoundIndex=1}^{\FederatedRoundCount(\LocalEpochCount)} \RoundDuration(\LocalEpochCount). \]

Each \(\RoundDuration(\LocalEpochCount)\) is one instance of the round budget sketched at the opening of the chapter. Read through the Fleet Law, it is the local training on the slowest client the round still awaits, plus the upload, plus the wait for enough reports, less whatever overlaps. The communication piece is easy to estimate. Over a constrained mobile uplink of 1–10 Mb/s, the chapter’s reference adapter update of 50 MB takes about 7 minutes to upload at the low end and about 42 seconds at the high end. That cost recurs in every one of the \(\FederatedRoundCount(\LocalEpochCount)\) rounds. A setting of \(\LocalEpochCount\) that halves \(\FederatedRoundCount(\LocalEpochCount)\) therefore removes half of the cumulative upload time. It pays off only if the extra local training it adds to each round, on the slowest awaited client, costs less than the uploads it removes. Client drift keeps that comparison from being settled on paper, because more local steps can also raise \(\FederatedRoundCount(\LocalEpochCount)\), and only measured runs can rank the settings.

A useful experiment log records local epochs, completed clients, rounds to target, elapsed time, transmitted bytes, and device energy. If a run never reaches the quality target within the budget, report that outcome rather than extrapolating an invented convergence curve. Repeated runs and cohort-level validation distinguish a stable improvement from a fortunate sample of participating devices.

Communication-computation trade-off

Local work \(\LocalEpochCount\) acts on the wall clock through two opposing paths, drawn as a timeline of rounds in figure 11. Increasing local epochs lengthens per-round execution duration \(\RoundDuration(\LocalEpochCount)\) (the width of each round) while reducing synchronization frequency (the number of rounds in a row), which can lower the required rounds \(\FederatedRoundCount(\LocalEpochCount)\) until client drift penalizes convergence. Both paths combine into the cumulative time to target \(\TimeToTarget(\LocalEpochCount)\), where each row ends, which remains admissible only if total consumption fits within client energy and fleet-coverage budgets. A setting that requires fewer rounds can still prolong total wall-clock duration. Selecting \(\LocalEpochCount\) therefore requires comparing measured times to target against physical admission constraints, rather than relying on ungrounded heuristics.

The operating point depends on the deployment. Larger local updates can amortize transfer overhead, but they also change drift, completion time, and which clients finish. The network alone can move the optimum, as a cost model in relative units shows (figure 12). Its minimum \(\LocalEpochCount^*\) shifts from \(\LocalEpochCount=10\) on the fast-network curve to \(\LocalEpochCount=40\) on the slow-network curve because amortizing expensive transfers justifies more local work. These curves are not measured convergence results. A deployment must establish whether additional local work actually reduces time to target after drift and client completion are counted, and adaptive epoch policies need the same outcome and coverage checks as fixed settings.

Diagnosing a missed target

Federated convergence results depend on the update rule, objective, sampling policy, and assumptions about client data (Kairouz and McMahan 2021). Heterogeneity, participation, and local sample counts are therefore inputs to a feasibility assessment, not universal pass/fail thresholds. A deployment must demonstrate that its chosen configuration reaches the declared quality target within time and device budgets, with evaluation that represents the intended population.

When a configuration misses that target, the measured failure guides the next experiment. Conflicting local updates motivate testing less local work, adding proximal regularization (FedProx), incorporating control variates (SCAFFOLD), or switching to personalization. Long waits motivate scheduling or asynchronous alternatives, subject to privacy-protocol compatibility. Sparse local evidence motivates bounded adaptation and stronger validation. None of these changes replaces observations from absent cohorts; each must be reevaluated for quality, resource cost, and population coverage.

The aggregation rule specifies how compatible local models produce a shared model. The measured time to target establishes whether a configuration reaches its objective within budget. A controlled lab can hold participant availability and network conditions steady. A phone fleet cannot, so the implementation must define how selection, deadlines, and failures change the set of updates that actually enters each aggregate. These protocol decisions connect learning behavior to fleet orchestration.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In the Federated Averaging (FedAvg) algorithm, what is the primary algorithmic trade-off when tuning the number of local training epochs (\(\LocalEpochCount\)) on client devices?

    1. Increasing local work can amortize communication, but may increase client drift on non-IID data; rounds and elapsed time to the same quality target must be measured.
    2. Increasing \(\LocalEpochCount\) forces the central server to store full training datasets from all participating clients in server DRAM.
    3. Increasing \(\LocalEpochCount\) eliminates the need for clients to compute backward passes during local training.
    4. Increasing \(\LocalEpochCount\) increases the size (in megabytes) of the transmitted model parameter vector by a factor of \(\LocalEpochCount^2\).

  2. A mobile federated learning deployment encounters severe client drift due to extreme non-IID typing patterns across users, causing standard FedAvg to oscillate and fail to converge. Which algorithmic modification directly addresses client drift by constraining local updates to stay close to the global model?

    1. Switching from 32-bit floating-point to 1-bit stochastic sign quantization without error compensation.
    2. Adding a proximal regularization term \(\frac{\mu}{2} \|w - w_t\|^2\) to the local client objective (as in FedProx) to penalize excessive deviation from the global server model \(w_t\).
    3. Increasing the local learning rate by \(10\times\) to accelerate convergence on outlier devices.
    4. Dropping the central aggregation server and allowing devices to train purely in isolation without synchronization.

  3. Contrast cross-silo federated learning and cross-device federated learning across three dimensions: participant scale/reliability, network connectivity, and client incentive/governance.

  4. In federated optimization, the phenomenon where multiple local gradient steps on non-identically distributed client data cause local models to diverge toward disparate local optima is called client ____.
